OPEN-AIR POLITICS.

THE GOVERMENT'S WORK FOR

LABOUR.

The Budget Supported.

Enthusiasm in the Progressive Labour League's open- s& campaign continues to be well maintained, and although the weather conditions for Monday evening's meeting near Arpley Cannons were somewhat depress- ing, there was a largeattendance, and Mr. J. Havelock 'Wilson, M.P., who paid a second visit, was listened to with great interest. Mr. G. Walker presided and was supported on the platform by Mr. J. Havelock Wilson, M.P„ Mr. E. Wynne-Junes, Mr. J. Howes, Mr. S. Wright; Messrs. J. Morton and J. Howes, jun. (hon. secretaries). The CHAIRMAN said they were told that the tariff reformers were making converts. That might be so to a certain extent, but those converts, they feared, took tariff reform very much in the same way that they took the measles—by contagion and not by conviction. (Laughter and applause). He asked the workingmen to consider the people who were advocating tariff reform. They did not find amongst them a member of the Labour party, whether extreme Socialist or moderate in his -views, supporting this policy which he thought that sixty years ago our forefathers discarded for ever. They could do with some of the advocates ok tariff reform if they would be consistent, but when they found them advocating on a platform that foreign goods should not be imported and at the same time going abroad for motor cars, and for the Cnristmas numbers of their publications it infde one rather jealous of those people. He believed firmly that if the Conservative paty entered office to-morrow tariff reform would be very much like old age pensions—they would, have to wait many years before they saw it brought into operation, because its supporters would realize the extreme difficulty of meet- ing the several phases of trade that came under their .notice. (Applause). Mr.J. HAVELOCK WILSON, M.P.,said it spoke well for the Radicalism of Warrington that even on a night like that they had got such a decent audience. Dealing with the Labour policy of the Liberal Gov.ernment, Mr. 'Wilson said be was first elected to Parliament in 1i92 and he had an experience from 1892 to 1895 of what a Liberal Government could accomplish for the working- men. He ventured to say that in that short Parliament they accomplished more for the working people than had been accomplished for any tweety years prior to that. In 1895 they had a Conservative Government returned and he well remembered that election and the many vague promises that were held out. As soon as the Conservatives were returned to office they forgot all about the promises they had made to the workingmen. For instance they beard in the 1895 election a great deal about old age pensions. Mr. Joseph Chamberlain was ore cot the principal exponents of that idea. He said : "We don't want to trouble ourselves about Home Rule for Ireland. What we want is social reform, such as old age pensions. "It was true that the Conservative Government did proceed to deal with pensions, but it was not for the working- men that they provided them. It was for the land- lords, the parsons and the squires. They were the first who had a call, and from 1895 right up to the year 1900 the Conservative party devoted their time more or less entirely to the interests of the upper classes. Not only that, but they landed us into one of the most extrava- gant wars that we had had probably for sixty or seventy years. When we talked of DEPRESSION OF TRADE

we had to thank the Coaservative Government for nll the trouble we were having in that respect, because be wanted to impress upon his workingmen friends the fact that if they spent £250,000,000 on a war, whether it was right or wrong, they might depend upon it that the country which had -t,o find • suchila sum to provide fireworks would inevitably antler, and the workingclasses were bound to suffer in consequence of the enormous waste of capital which took place in Soeth Africa. In 10(0 the Cqn-, servative Government appealed to the cciintry on the grounds that they desired to clear up that war. They knew how they "did clear it up and what state of affairs they left in South Africa, and it was fortunate for this country and for the world at large that the Liberals were returned in 1906. They were left with a _legacy from the Tories .and had to put matters right in South Africa. To-day peace and contentment reigned in South Africa, racial hatred which the Tory Govern- ment was responsible for stifling up was now dying down, and he believed that in another five or six years, if the Liberals could be retained in office, they would indeed have a prosperous and successful South Africa and a revival in trade in this country that few people anticipated. (Applause). How far had the Liberal Government redeemed their promises ? First of all they had an extension of the . WORKMEN'S COMPENSATION ACT

which bad brought within the scope of that measure from four to five millions of people who were lett out- side by the Tory Government. Almost every 'class of workingmen—and workingwomen too—was to-day receiving the protection and benefit of that Act end the class he represented—the sea-faring men—owed a -very deep debt of gratitude to the Government for the extension of that Act to the men who "go down to the sea in ships." Almost every day of his life he met with scores of cases showing the benefit of the Act and only that day, before he left Liverpool, he had five or six cases in hand, some in which men had lost, their eyesight, others in which they had lost the use of their limbs through injury.ln the old days not one penny of compensation would have been paid to those men 'whereas to-day,although they were permanently injured, they would be able to look forward to the fact tbat they would receive from their employers or the Insurance societies 50 percent. of their earnings, which would keep them at least outside the poorhouse and save the rates to that extent. In addition to the Work- men's Compensation Act, the Liberal Government introduced a measure of far-reaching importance to sea-faring people— THE MERCHANT SHIPPING ACT

which had conferred great privileges and protection on them. Mr. Wilson dealt at length with the main principles of the Act which, amongst other thing., ,cnacts a greatly improved scale of provisions for sailors, snakes it impossible for a ship owner to withhold a man's wages to pay for his maintenance in hospital and conveyance home if be is seized with illness in foreign ports, gives the right of appeal from the decisions of Naval Courts, and in case of a dispute as to the amount of wages payable, a sailor is now able to sign a form "under protest," draw the whole of the wages not in dispute, and proceed to recover the balance due to him. Under the old conditions men frequently found them- selves in such a plight that in order to receive any wages at all they had to sign a document foregoing any claim upon the owner, and in that way seamen had been done out of thousands of pounds in a year. Did his hearers think for a moment that if a Conservative Government had been in office they would haNe accepted any such amendments as the present Govern- ment accepted from him in order to make the measure of the fullest benefit to the seamen ? He was certain they would not. He knew that the forces on the side of the Tory party would have been so great that even if the Minister in charge of the Bill had been prepared to consider the proposals, they would not have been accepted in consequence of the opposition of gentle- men on the Conservative side. There was not a work- ingman in the country to-day who could honestly say that the Government had not brought about great reforms for every section of Labour. Whether they, were miners, workers in factories or shops, or in what- ever other employment they might he engaged, he ventured to say no Government had ever been itroffice that bad done so much for the workingpeople.

(Applause). At the present time they had to face a very formidable fight—a strenuous struggle it would Le—over

THE BUDGET.

The Tory party described the Budget as pure, unadulterated Socialism. if there were any Socialists present they ought to feel proud of that fact. (Laughter). What was Mr. Lloyd-George proposing ? First of all they had established the principle of old- age pensions. He (the speaker) 'would like to ask any Conservative or Socialist if he had any fault to find with that. He took it they were all agreed that it was a beneficent measure, and one that was due to the wage earners of this country. Having established tht fact that they should give old-age pensions, they had to provide the money, and as far as be could see there was no class in the community which was not called upon by the Budget to pay its fair share of the cost. There was one thing he would like to say to the well-to-do people—that the workinguasn who was doing his share in providing the pensions had not kicked against it.. (Hear, hear.) When they were smoking their pipes they knew they were doing a pit for old age pensions. The working people had honestly faced the music. It was the wealthy who were howling. They said they would have to give up some of their luxuries. He (the speaker) hear(' one of his Labour friends, Mr. George Nicholls, M.P., in referring to some of the well-to-do gentlemen who were ,bewailing the fact that they'had to pay an additional income tax on £lO,OOO a year, said "Well, look here, my good friends, 1 have got a very small salary and I don't know that I pay any income tax at all, but if you are in great trouble I will change places with you." (Laughter). Needless to say, no change took place. One moment these gentlemen were calling upon the country to provide eight Dread- noughts, but when it came to a question of paying for them they sang another song altogether. If he (Mr. Wilson) were struck on war ships, it would ill become him if be objected to paying his fair share to provide them, and the people who were bellowing for war ships, strong armies and navies, ought not to object to a Budget such as the one now before the House of Commons, because he thought it was a fair and just Budget. There was one point that many of them seemed to be hard hit upon—

THE TAXATION OF LAND VALUES.

He (the speaker) could not see where that proposal was unjust in the least degree. He had seen instances quite recently where the community had spent thousands of pounds in improving a district, and the land adjoining those improvements had increased in value, not in con- sequence of anything that the holder of the land had done, but in consequence of the improved surroundings. If ever there was a just and righteous proposal, it WWI that if the community at large improved the value of land, the man who was reaping the benefit of it should be called upon to pay a fair share of that value back to the community. (Applause). It behoved working- men to see that whatever happened, the Liberal Govern- meat should not go do.vn on a Budget like that before the House at the present moment. When Sir William ' Harcourt introauced the Death Duties, the Tories , prophesied dire calamity; the country would not survive the shock for one year. (Laughter). It was in 1893 that the Death Duties were brought in, and the eapital had not left the country yet. (Laughter). We were increasing in value year after year. If these gentlemen went to Germany or France, they would have to pay more than they did in this country, so that all the talk about capital leaving the country was pure nonsense, without a word of foundation. Although the Conservative Party denounced the Death Duties, they did not take any steps to repeal them when they came into office. They went on collaring all they could get and they found it was a jolly good thing tor revenue. ( Laughter). They were stirring up opposition in the House of Commons now to lead the people to believe that, a grave injustice was being done, lie hoped that Mr. Lloyd-George would stand by every bit of his Budget. (Applause). There might be some bard cases which required careful consideration and Mr. Lloyd. George had promised that if they were brought to his notice they would receive it, but be (the speaker) sincerely hope that the Government would stand by the Budget as a whole and that the workingmen would demand that they should stand by it. The working people of this country were paying. a larger proportion of their incomes than the wealthy men, so he did not think the latter had much to complain of. He was proud to notice the step the Government took last week in calling the party together with a view to going round the country, explaining that this was a workingman's Budget and that the workingpeople must stand by it. (Applause,. The Tories told us that the course pro- posed in the Budget was an altogether wrong principle, and that whit we ought to do to get revenue was to tax the foreigner. But when they started to tax the foreigner they would find the process was not quite as simple as it appeared. He did not believe they thought fora moment that they could, get the money out of the foreigner. They were more concerned with throwing the whole burden on to the undenelog, the working- man, and that was what, in his opinion, tariff reform would do. He (the speaker) 'night -be asked why be called himself a Liberal-Labour man and not an inde- pendent Labour man. He did not see how a man 'could be independent, because he noticed in the House of Commons that there were only two division lobbies, and when it came to a vote he generally had with him in the same lobbby the men who called themselves independent Labour men. But when it came to a question of taking up an independent atti- tude in the House of Commons, he thought his record would bear favourable comparison with that of any man who called himself an independent Labour man. Mr. Wilson instanced his opposition to Mr. Winston Churchill's proposals for Labour exchanges, remarking that if they did no more for workingmen on shore than they had done for the seamen, he had very little confidence in them. Labour Exchanges were not going to make employment for the men who were out of work. The idea was that they ;would be the means of giving information to workingmen as to where employment could he obtained, but his experience was that the workingmen generally found that out for him- self, without depending on Government officials. Mr. Wilson -referred to the possibility that the important offices in connection with Labour Exchanges would be filled by University men, the sons of the aristocracy,who had been failures in other walks of life. He sincerely trusted he might be wrong, because if that class were going to be pitched into these offices, just for the purpose of finding them employ- ment, he said "God help the workingman who has got, to depend on them." He did not care whether it was a Liberal or a Tory Government that was in office; if itproposed legislation, which in his honest judgment, would not be beneficial to the people he represented, he would speak against it, regardless of party ties. In spite of that fact he was a Liberal-Labour man, because the workers owed the Liberal party so much for the many reforms they had brought about. Explaining why he was not a Socialist, Mr. Wilson said he wanted the State to do certain things which properly and rightfully belonged to the' State, but he did not believe in the State interfering with the rights and privileges of the people. if all the means of production and distribution were in the bands of the State, were well all going to have the same reward, and get the same amount? Would the gifted and expert workman be prepared to share out with the man who could not do as much in a day as he (the expert) could in an hour? If they got down to the idea of the State taking over everything, he ventured to predict that if ever that day came they would find that the gentlemen who were going to run the show and manipulate the division, would have two for themselves and leave one for the other fellow— (laughter)—because of the extra responsibilities which were placed on their shoulders. While they could not have equality, be was in favour of every man in the community having an equal opportunity and also a fair share of the wealth he helped to create. (Applause.) Mr. Wilson concluded with a reference to the House , of Lords and urged the working classes not to allow a Chamber which represented only privilege and monopoly

to dictate to them as to what class of legislation they would allow their representatives in the House of Commons to pass. (Applause.) . .

Mr. E. VaYNNE ..l&iEs then moved a resolution approving of the Budget and expressing the hope that it would be carried through in its entirety. He said that if they were to judge by the opposition to the Budget, they would be drawn to the conclusion that the Liberal Party was springing some surprise upon the country but so far from springing a surprise in dealing with the land, the Government were only fulfilling the promises made by the Party leader. When Sir Henry Campbell-Bannerman submitted his policy at the great Albert Hall meeting before the General Election he said: We desire to develop our undeveloped estates in this country, to colonise our own country, to give the ' farmer greater freedom and greater security in the exercise of his busiiieSs ; to secure a home and a career for the labourer, who is now in many cases cut off from, the soil. We wish to make the land less of a pleasure ground for the rich and more of a treasure house for the nation." To the taxation of land the Government were pledged. Mr. Cobden said in 1864 'You can do more for your country in liberating the land than has already been done by the liberation of its trade." Instead of revoking Free Trade we must go on to free the land,l or land was the primary element in the production of all wealth. Without the land, labour and capital were of no use, and our present system hampered and cramped our trade and industry. In many ways the Budget weuld do lasting good for, the workers._ (Applause). _ _ . . .

Mr. J 'HowFs seconded. He reinarkeci that one noble Duke—the Duke of Marlborough, was sorely pressed, and when he (Mr. Howes) read the report of what he had said, it almost brought tears to his eyes ILaughter). The Duke said : "lie was asked to pay a shilling in the pound for a lot of money he did not ' get. He was now going to be asked to pay a cupertax ' upon that money which he did not get. If he kept his land he was going to pay a penny in the pound if he did not develop it. If somebody else did that for him, ' he had to pay 10 per cebt. N‘hen he was thoroughly weary and exhausted with the struggle of trying to live at all, and when in desperation he threw himself out of a window, his successor would have the privilege of paying 15 or 20 per cent. or 25 percent. for that right to retire into another world. (Laughter). He would leave his successor with an impoverisheld and curtailed prpperty to carry on under the same difficult and impossible conditions. He thought. the result of the Budget would be that land would have to be administered by public bodies, That would mean a bureauciacy, and those who lived on the soil would be very much worse off than under the good and kind rule of landlordism which existed to-day." (Laughter). The Duke said he was going to pay on money he had never got, but there was no provision in the Budget to make hi m do anything of the kind. First the Budget said it must be proved that he had got a lot, and from that lot he bad to pay very little. The land tax was extremely small and light, hut they hailed it with enthusiasm because it was the beginning of a grand system. (Applause). Whatever might be Mr. Lloyd-George's ides, the land reformers' idea was that they accepted the Budget proposals as'an Instalment, the commence- ment of the taxation of land values, in order that those who reaped a huge advantage from the people should return to the State part of the plunder. It was a glorious thing to remember that when these wealthy men had no longer any use for their money, the State would take a little of it. If the man who came into possession of the estate was grieved, there were thousands who would change places with hill. (Laughter.) The landowners could weep and , gnash their teeth and wring their hands but reformers had no consolation to offer them. (Applause.) Such whinings of the Dukes would only stimulate them to greater activity. (Applause.) _ _ _

. _ The resolution was carried with enthusiasm. A hearty vote of thanks was passed to Mr. Havelock Wilson on the proposition of Mr. J. Morton.

BEAUTIFUL WARRINGTON SOCIETY.

Open-Air Concert.

School Brow is not an ideal place for a concert, if the music is to be appreciated in any degree of comfort,but it served the purpose of the Beautiful Warrington Society on Monday evening when they heist the second of a series of open-air concerts. Thesociety had secured the services of Misses J. Armstrong, C. Newal I and E. Taylor, and Messrs. F. Fieldhouse and J. H. Draper and their various contributions delighted the large audience whose hcpes in the early part of the evening seemed likely to be flouted by the inclement weather. As the concert went on the conditions greatly improved, and with this the enthusiasm of the audience. The Bev. Harvey-Cook presided, and accompanying him on the platform, were: —Mrs. Harvey-Cook, Miss Harvey-Cook,Ma.ster Harvey- Cook, Alderman A. 13enuett, Mr. W. Mason, Mr. J. Long land, the artistes, and a number of the young life of the district.

Thf., CHAIRMAN opened the proceedings with the song "A lilt on the way," the andience joining in the chorus. Mr. Draper rendered "Once again," in a way which earned the plaudits of the crowd. This was followed by Miss Armstrong, who sang " My am n folk" with fitting expression. The audience was quite captiv- ated by the quartette "The budding bloom of May," which appeared to be quite novel in such surroundings. "Corning through the rye," was interpreted by Miss Taylor in her usual tasteful style. Then Mr. Field- house's turn catne, and he was soon popular with his listeners, for he sang "Four jolly tmiths," and received an encore. "Bonnie Mary of Argyle" by Mr. Draper was evidently a favourite, judging from the appreciation of the crovl d.

Mr. MASON said they were not there to give them advice. They had plenty of that, hadn't they? (Cries of "yes !") He wanted to ask them for their help. Everyone of them ;could help them in the work they had so closely at heart. We did not want to make the town beautiful every 300 years when the King came to *visit us; but to have it always years, so that the King could come every day of his life. Parents could do a great deal for IA arrington. They had their children at home, end they could give them plenty of advice, when the teachers had done with them at school. They tried to make them understand the beautiful things of Nature, end they wanted the parents to teach the children to take care of them. He had much pleasure in proposing a vote of thanks to the chairman' the artistes, and 'to the members of the Beautiful Warrington Society,.. who were giving them "a little help ,on the way.' (Applause).

• Alderman BE?.NETT in seconding, said he hoped the audience had enjoyed themselves; they wanted them to enjoy themselves. The •speaker then asked those who were members of the society to raise their bands, and there were only a few children who were not mernbers.lle said it was very gratifying to see so many were members, but he should like to see them all members. There were some very beautiful faces there—even in School Brow. (Hear hear). It was not much like a garden. there, for them to live in. He did not like to see such pretty faces in the dark surroundings. School Brow was a very ugly and squalid part of the town. They wanted them to like something better, and they wanted them as they grew up and got votes to do what they could to make the town beautiful. They could send men to the Coun- cil who would make the town beautiful. To those who had votes he asked if they were satisfied with School- brow. (Cries of " Yes ' and "No.") They wanted to make them all so dissatisfied that they would never be content whilst there was a single slum in Warrington. Looking up at one of the windows on which rested a box of flowers, the speaker said he hoped the next time he went along School-brow he would see a box of flowers in every window. The vote of thanks was carried with enthusiasm.

The concluding songs were by Miss Mayor, who sang "On the banks of Alan Water," and Miss Armstrong " Killarney." The concert terminated with the singing of "God save the King."MissC.Newall accompanied on the piano in her usual characteristic style.

WARRINGTON DISTRESS COMMITTEE A Year's Work Reviewed.

A meeting of the Warrington Distress Committee was held at the Town Hall on Wednesday evening, Alderman Tinnion presiding. There were also present: —Mrs. Simpson, the Rev. W. Bracecamp, Councillors Wilkinson, Wilson, Dickenson And Broadhurst, Messrs. A. J. Jolley, J. Haley, W. C. Brooks, T. P. Bradshaw, T. H. Spinks (assistant town clerk), and ,J. Fargher (inquiry officer).

===== 0002601_19090703_art0019.txt | included=true | included =====
FINANCIA L

At a meeting of the. Finance and General Purposes Committee, the report of the Assistant, District Auditor (Mr. Arthur E. Ellis) was submitted and showed that the expenditure for the year was £154 10s.-9d. and that. there was now a balance in hand of £214 us. 8. Councillor WILKINSON, in moving the adoption of the minutm, said that in view of the fact that there aas such a large balance in hand the Committee pro- posed to ask for only £5O this year. Win answer to Councillor Dickenson, Councillor entuussos stated that they commenced the year just tied with £167 17s. sd. in hand. The Rev. W. 1311ACECAMP seconded.

The Rev. J. HALEY said that before the next annual meeting the new Act would probably have done away with their work under the present aegis, and the new scheme would be financed by the State. The minutes were approved. THE YEAR'S WORK. INQUIRY OFFICER'S SALARY RAISED.

At a meeting of the Inquiry and Labour Committee, the annual report of the officer was submitted and con- sidered generally satisfactory. It stated that a regret- able increase in the number of persons registered during the year was shown but it was satisfactory to note that though trade had been depressed the number for whom employment was found was maintained. During the year, April Ist, 1908, to March 31st, 1909, applications for employment were received from 'J33 men,352 youths 489 women, 250 girls, or a total of 2,021, being an increase of 225 over the total for the previous year, the figures for which were :-975 men, 287 youths, 354 women, 183 girls, total, 1,799. The increase was due to more youths and women registering. The number of situations offered by employers during the year was 707, and the number of situations filled was 592, of which 304 were permanent and 288 temporary, as compared with 580 situations filled the previous year. The situations offered and not filled numbered 115, the majority being for domestic servants. Those for whom employment was found were again mainly of unskilled occupations, though there was a much larger percentage of better class situations offered through the agency of the Labour Bureau than formerly. Employers now appeared to be recognising the utility of the bureau, and that it was carried on with the object of being a useful medium where their requirements were met, if possible, and that at the same time employees were recommended who were more or less qualified to fill the vacancies offered The inquiry officer expresses the view that if the authorities set up under the Unemployed Workmen Act, and the Town Council (who are the financiers), bad, less restrictions placed upon them and were given wore freedom of action, good results would obtain. What ought to be borne in mind by the Government in dealing with this problem was that the same condi- tions did not answer the different parts of the country, as in each they were as varying as the human frame, and, to use a simile, the cloth whiah ought to be pro- vided by the State should not be cut by State machinery to fit all alike, but should be sent down to the different districts, where the wearer would obtain a better fit by a tailor who was on the spot and knew the measurements.

The committee resolved that the salary of the inquiry officer ber increased from 30s. to £2 per week and further that a grant of .210 he made to him for the extra services during the past, six months. Mr. A. J. JOLLEY, in moving the adoption of the minutes, said Mr. Fargher had done admirable work for the committee and the increase of salary would be a grateful recognition of loyal and efficient service. Mr. WELKE:ism' in seconding, also bore testimony to the enthusiastic way in which Mr.Fargher had done his duty. At, the outset the committee had misgivings as to whether they would be able to do a great amount of work, but they had endeavoured to do their best. _

Mr. BRADSIIAw, in supporting, said the report of the iuquiry_offieer spoke well for his abilities.

;Ir. -HALEY said an enormous arm:mat of work passed through Mr. Farghei's bands during the period of distress but it was efficiently done. Although a fair amount of relief was distributed, he (Mr. Haley) was sorry itiwas not more.

The CHAIRMAN said Mr. Fargher was an efficient officer inadequately paid and they now proposed to pay him a salary more in keep with his position and value. Thq scheme which the committee originally adopted 114 S so drawn up as to work automatically, with the careful supervision of an officer, and the work had been done so well that it had been unnecessary to call the committee together often. The minutes were approved. THE WORK OF THE REGISTER.

A return was submitted showing that the number of persons registered for employ ment since the opening of the bureau at Christmas, 1905, was 5,973 and the number for whom employment was found was 1701.

MR. CROSFIELD AND THE BUDGET.

A FIGHT AGAINST MONOPOLY, PRIVILEGE AND SELFISHNESS.

Mr. Crosfield, M.P., addressed the following letter on the Budget to 'Mr- Wynne:Jones, in view of last Monday evening's Progressive Labour League meeting at Bridge Foot :

39. Hyde Park Gate, S.W. 27th June, 1909

Dear Mr. Wynne-Jones,---I learn with very great pleasure of your intention to have a resolution, in support of Ile Budget, moved at the meeting of the Progressive Labour League to-morrow. Week by week I have followed in tile " Examiner," the admir- able and very interesting series of meetings held by the Progressive Labour League in the splendid. fight which the League has been putting up in defence of Free Trade. It strikes me our friends, the Tariff Reformers, must regret they ever started in Warring- ton a campaign in which they have been so thoroughly well beaten by the League. And now besides the attack on Free Trade, Liberals and Progressives are face to face with an equally vehement attacil on the Budget, an attack made by those who, while they admit the necessity for raising the additional revenue, admittedly seek to substitute for the Chancellor's proposals taxes on the ' prime necessaries of life.

From the meeting of Financiers held this last week in the City, from the threatened action of the House of Lords, from any amount of evidence inside the House.of Commons and out of it, it is perfectly plain that the forces and influences in the country which stand for monopoly, privilege, and, I, feel bound to add, often for mere selfishness as well, are going to make a very determined effort to defeat the Budget proposals of the Government. And so last Wednes- day, in the large committee room which adjoins the historic old Westminster Hall, wehad a great meeting of the party, at which it was decided to form a Budget League with Haldane as President and Winston Churchill as chairman, the object being to arrange to hold meetings all Over the country to educate and organise public 'opinion in support of the Budg/t. Sir Henry Norman is Secretary of the League, and last night I received from him a letter asking one's opinion as to the advisability of holding a meeting or meetings in Warrington, suggesting that we might like to have down a Speaker and so forth. I have at once written to ir Henry thanking him for the assistance he offers and telling him that we shall gladly avail ourselves of it, mentioning also that we have ourselves lost no time in giving the Government strong support for the Budget, as a meeting is to be held for ti at very purpose in the constituency to- morrow. I felt sure you would not think I was over- stepping the mark in saying that much to Sir Henry Norman, because I cannot for one moment doubt that the resolution in support of the Budget will receive the very earnest, hearty and sincere approval of the meeting to-morrow. I feel sure of that for the follow- ing reasons. First, because the money is needed both for the purposes of Imperial defence and social reform. Secondly, because the Budget provides it and because it provides a margin, too, which will enable us to proceed with beneficent social reform in the future. Thirdly, because the money is raised in a manner fair to all classes of the community. Fourthly, because it taxes—and taxes quite moderately I think— the unearned increment of site values in our towns and. cities and in their immediate vicinities. That is the principle of taxation which some of us have supported for many a year past, and so is the principle of graduated 'taxation, a principle which is further and substantially developed by the super tax on incomes exceeding £5,000 a year. May I venture to offer that extension of the principle of graduation in our system of taxation as an important reason for the powerful democratic support which lam wire you will agree with me that the Budget deserves. We had a stiff fight at the last election in defence of Free Trade—a stiff fight and a great and memorable victory for a great and good cause. At that time we made it clear enough from a score of platforms, that the great Liberal Party would not be content with any mere negative policy in support of Free Trade ; that we had a long overdue programme of positive work to carry out as well. I am sure if my friend Mr*. Havelock Wilson makes any reference to that to-morrow, he will give ample proof that this pro- gressive energetic government has in no way been behindhand in Working for those very causes which the Progressive Labour League has, from the outset, put in the very forefront of its programme. And if the Government has already a splendid record of work behind it, and equally good and necessary work to carry out in the near future, I am sure Mr. Wilson will ask the League to give the Budget emphatic and unflinching support, both to enable us to carry out all this work, and because the Budget is itself such a fine piece of democratic statesmanship. . .

Yes, Mr. Wyrm-e-Jones, and whatever oppositiOn is offered and publicly threatened whether by financiers in the City, or territorial magnates in the Lords, we hare got to carry- it, and we will. And as in the case of Free Trade ; of Old Age Pensions, and the supplementary contributory scheme which is to follow them, and so much besides of the work this Government has done, so when we have carried the Budget, I think that also will be a piece of work which you and I and every member of the League will be able in future years to look back upon with deep and permanent satisfaction.—l remain, faithfully and sincerely yours,

A. H. CROSFIELD.

Warrington Woman's Sudden Death.

An inquest was held at the Hall Cafe, Stockton Heath,. on Tuesday evening, by Mr. Thomas Ridgway, touching the death of Ellen Lawrinson, widow, aged 63 years, residing ot 1, Brick-street, Warrington. The evidence showed that deceased had been in the employment of Mrs. Finlayson, Park-road, Stockton Heath, as a charwoman about eight years. She became ill about ten o'clock on Monday morning, complaining of pains in the chest and feet, and before medical aid arrived she went into the yard and fell on her face groaning. Mrs. Finlayson went to her and she was breathing very heavily. She afterwards expired. Dr. Howard said that in his opinion death was due to either heart disease or a stroke. The jury returned a verdict of" Death from natural causes."

NOTES ON GAMES, AND ATHLETICS.

Cricket.

Warrington made a splendid Lid for victoi v in their match with Prescot, but the fates were against them, and a draw was the outcome of the Contest. The importance attached to the engagement, which has considerable bearing on the League championship, was evidenced by the fact that the attendance numbered fully 800. Warrington won the toss and elected to bat first. They were in capital form and, as the result of sound all-round play, amassed 218 with only 7 wickets down. It was thought safe to declare the innings and devote all efforts to dismissing their opponents. The prospects of N.Actory for the Warring- tonians seemed rosy and excitement ran high when Prescot had their last two men in. Attempts to dis- lodge the wickets were, however, futile and Prescot were fortunate 'in saving the situation, for they had' n-ade only 131 for 9 wickets when play ceased.

While there was no tall. scoring on the Warrington side, several displays were of more than ordinary merit. S. Partridge, a smart young player, carried out his bat after making 38, and was warmly compli- mented upon his performance. B. Silccck, the War- rington captain, wielded the bat w:th his usual good judgment and placed 39 to his credit. Cresswell,the pre- fesstonal, was in dashing form and opened his account with five fours. He drove w :th remarkable power and made 34 not out. T. Wright played sparkling cricket his chief hits including seven 4's.

He played all the bowling f -eely, and obtained all his runs with remlrkable rapidity, but eventually a fast ball from the Rev. Horace Gray disturbed his middle stwup. Mr. Gray bcwled with deadly effect throughout and captured 6 wickets for 43 ions.

Warrington 2nd experienced their first reverse of the season on Saturday at the hands of Prescot 2nd. The visitors gave the Warringtonians a lively time leather-hunting, and ran up a total of 151. In reply Warrington made orly 66, W. V. Tinton (19) being their only batsman to attain double figures. For the whiners W. H. Mercer batted brilliattly for 47.

WHITECROSS WARD ELECTION.

Mr. Henry Roberts Returned Unopposed.

Monday was the last day for receiving nominations to fill the casual vacancy in this ward occasioned by the election of Corncillor James Evans to an alder- manic seat. Mr. Henry Roberts, Kingsley, Cliffe, who stood as an independent, was the only oanclidat3 nominated and he was therefore returned unopposed.

A SONG OF WELCOME.

The King is coming to the town, And fain am I to greet him With words befitting his renown, And with a song to meet him ; No warmer welcome will he find In any place, I swear it; The flags which float on every wind, The eager crowds, declare it. The Queen is coming to the town; She brings the sunshine with her; And Summer well may cease to f,-own As they ride here together ; From seventy thousand throats will rica A rapturous stm al of cheering ; Tice. seventy thousand eager eyes Will watch their chariot nearing.

The Princess too is coming ! May Her life be long and surny ; For her the land be always g-ty, And flow with milk and honey; Three changing oratories blve passcd Since James the First rode hither ; And, now, with joyous hearts, at last, We greet the three together. We bid them welcome to the pla?e Where Roman ttumpeis sor•ided ; Whose church was built in Saxon days, Whose fairs the NoLnans founded ; Whence noble kn;ghts rode forth to war, Which gallant earls defended, And on whose forehead, like a star, The light of Fame descended. And as, in thought, I see them iide Through lanes of happy faces, From every window, open wide, Some ghost of memmy gazes; The streets are changing evevwhere, The ancient houses falling, But mystic voices in the air From evey stone are calling. " God save the li;ne• ! God rave the Queen God save the towo.—we love it ! God save our island home serene, And' may no tempest move it ; God save the swelling empire vast They rude with wise endeavor-. And bind its utmost islands fast In bonds of love for ever !"

The streets are full ; from yorng and old The eel rsame ell is ring,n7, As out into the sunset's gold They ri;learr;d the sinl;ng. Our love goes with them on the;r way; Heaven send-them golden weather; We shall not soon forget the day When they rode here together ! 6th July 1909, • ARTIWIZ

It 1)141 'e ViSable for men engaged in any kind of kle:Da transaction to place themselves and themselves, in an agreeable Sand

===== 0002601_19090703_art0050.txt | included=false | skipped/gap: fewer than 20 non-control characters after cleanup =====
-

===== 0002601_19090703_art0051.txt | included=true | included =====
honest relationship to their customers. If you bully your customer, he will go to another shop. If you secretly cheat him, he will do the same thing when he finds you out—or something worse. If you openly cheat him (which means taking him for a fool) he may probably hit back. Now, "the Trade," or a large portion of it, took to open tyranny over the public, not because it wanted to injure its customers,but because it desired to make a great case against the Budget. Prices went up with a bound far beyond anything that the Budget could justify. A man who drunk ever so little could not get his drink at a reasonable price, less because the Budget had made spirits somewhat d6,rer than because "the Trade" had determined to disgust the public with Mr. LLOYD-GEORGE. It was obvious from the beginning that this excessively unintelligent opposition to the financial pro- posals of the Government could not continue for very long. There were many dealers in licensed drinks who were sensi.ble enough to take the view that the policy adopted by most Brewery Trusts and firms of distillers and owners of scores and hundreds of licensed houses was a mistake. They were not tied to this or the other great undertakings, and they dealt with their customers as if they supposed them to have brains enough to understand what the Budget really meant. And they kept their trade therefore. If they gave a little less drink for the money, or a little more water to keep the quantity all right, they did not charge a penny for what cost them a farthing. And this is the kind of wisdom that has been justified of its children. These reasonable publicans, to whom a demonstration against the Budget seemed less important than the maintenance of their trade, have kept their customers, and have made their usual profits. These observations bring us to • our real subject. The demons trators against the Budget have climbed down. They climbed down on Wednesday. The glass of whisky that was raised from threepence to fourpence can once more be obtained for threepence. The various "specials "that had been raised to fivepence are down again to fourpence. This not only answers, but actually exceeds, our expectations. We never had any doubt that prices would rush down again as soon as the Budget was through, but we did believe that they would be kept up so long as the enhanced price could be made to serve any political purpose. We were mistaken, as we admit. It is evident that the man who drink also thinks. He has taken the measure of the movement against him, and has acted accordingly. For which reason, "the Trade," or perhaps we ought to say the men who have tried to dominate "the Trade," have, as we previously remarked, climbed down, and have no doubt learned a lesson also.

All hail to their Majes- ALL HAIL! ties! Warrington's welcome on Tuesday will reach the highest pinnacle of loyal enthusiasm. The townspeople are bubbling over with eagerness to raise a shout of welcome, and to demonstrate their affec- tionate regard for the gracious Monarch who presides over the destiny of this great Empire with such kingly dignity and statesmanlike wisdom. Their tribute of love and loyalty will know no measure, and only the presence of the Queen, whose tender sympathy for the humblest subject has for so long endeared her to every heart, can cause any division in the volume of popular acclaim. Long life, and health, and happiness to their Majesties is the prevailing sentiment, and it will be adequately expressed in the warmth of Warrington's welcome. ,

• Mr. Asquith has called THE RALLYING for a rally of the Party CRY. to the defence of the _ _ _

Budget in the great campaign which has just been inaugurated. His appeal will be answered with enthusiasm. As Mr. Winston Churchill says, there are great issues—financial, fiscal, imperial, and social—which depend upon the Budget. And if it should befall—though, in spite of Lord Rothschild's sinister suggestion that if there is clamour enough raised by the vested interests, "the Lords will do their duty," we will not suppose people bad until they prove to be so—that a great constitutional issue should overshadow all the rest, the courage and energy of the Liberal Party will be not daunted, but braced and stimulated, by the necessity of fighting for the supreme power in matteis of finance which the elected representatives of the people have enjoyed for 250 years. Mr. Crosfield, M.P., strikes the right note in a letter to the Progressive Labour League which we publish in another column. There is an inspiring tone about his reference to the Budget: "Whatever opposition is offered and publicly threatened," he says "whether by financiers in the City, or territorial magnates in the Lords, we have got to carry it and we will! " The Borough Member does not mince matters in indicating the nature of the opposition to Liberal reforms, and we feel sure that his constituents in Warrington will fully agree with him that the party is up against the forces of monopoly, privilege and selfishness. Never was this fact more clearly manifested than it is to-day by the fight for the Budget. The path of reform is blocked; the issue lies between pocket and progress; and it must be a source of great satisfaction to the democracy of the country to see the determined attitude of the Libergl and Labour parties in the crisis. They mean to brook no interference and, for ourselves, we have unshaken faith in the ultimate succes of right against might. The classes have flouted the masses too long and retribution looms stern and resistless on the horizon.

Mr. Havelock Wilson, LABOUR M.P., delivered a sound EXCHANGES. and convincing address at the open-air meeting on Monday evening. He is a man possess- ing a full title to speak for Labour and he made it abundantly clear to his hearers that the present Government has done more for the workers and the masses in its short but strenuous existence than has been achieved during the previous twenty years. Mr. Wilson has an honest respect for the Liberal legislature of to-day, but he is not a slavish worshipper at the shrine of party. While he admires the Government he does not consider it infallible, and he has made no secret of his contempt for the proposed Labour Exchanges. Mr. Wilson stands

===== 0002601_19090703_art0052.txt | included=true | included =====
almost alone in his disbelief in this latest scheme for lessening unemployment, but he is not to be thought any the less of on that account. We give him credit for the sturdi- ness of his convictions while we dissent from his judgment on Mr. Churchill's scheme. Mr. Wilson is a little at fault in condemning the Labour Exchanges before they have been tried, and it is necessary to remember that there is a large body of enlightened reformers who have faith in the sug- gested means for preventing the waste in work and labour which is continually going on through the want of a central informative and informing organisation. A White Paper issued this week dealing with the cost of Labour Exchanges should clear away many wild statements and esti- mates. Mr. Churchill calculates that 240 Exchanges will be required. These, with all necessary expenditure in -working, will cost about £lOO,OOO this year, and the charge will rise to a maximum of .£210,000 next year. As the permanent buildings are erected the amount saved on rent will be about £lO,OOO a year, and by 1919 the annual cost will have been reduced to £lBO,OOO. That is not too much to pay for machinery that by organising the supply of labour will probably reduce the annual loss in wages and on relief by many times the amount. We look for- ward to the setting up of these Exchanges with high hopes. They are not a solution of the unemployed problem, but they are the first and absolutely necessary step in finding a solution.

Two resignations CHANGES IN THE caused by ill-health MINISTRY, have necessitated a certain . amount of readjustment in the Ministry. Lord Fitz- maurice, who has thus been obliged, under imperative medical advice, to terminate a long and honourable career, has been a strenuous worker for the cause of Liberalism and of oppressed and suffering humanity in many parts of the world. Though his appointment to the office of Chancellor of the Duchy of Lancaster nominally severed his connection with the Foreign Office, he con- tinued to represent that Department in the House of Lords. It is twenty-nine years since he was placed on the Commission to recognize Crete and the European Provinces of Turkey; and forty years since he entered Parliament. Mr. T. R. Buchanan has done good work, first as Financial Secretary to the War Office, and then as Under-Secretary for India. It would be impossible to imagine any Cabinet which would not find a notable acquisition in a young statesman with the administrative capacity and debat- ing skill of Mr. Herbert Samuel. The Master of Elibank, who succeeds Mr. Buchanan at the India Office, was distinguished by the tact and geniality with which he performed the duties of Scottish Liberal Whip.

rNOTES 11.N‘D NOTIONS.

We suppose the Suffragettes will look upon thirteen as their lucky number, seeing that on Tuesday they had their thirteenth and record raid on the House of Commons. It was a record, and a success, because 122 raiders (mostly ladies) were arrested by the police. Mrs. Pankhurst smacked an inspector's face, but in most respects the demonstration was lacking in the dramatic sense. The attitude of the general public was one of amused tolerance rather than sympathy, and evidence is not lacking that the violent tactics of the raiders are doing the cause no good. The public is apt to tire of people whose main arguments appear to be noise and naughtiness. * * *

The wording of the loyal address from Warrington to the King appears to have given little satisfaction locally, judging from the severe strictures which we have heard passed upon it. Certainly there is nothing of an objectionable character about the wording; it is not even lacking in taste, but fsomething not quite so bald and colourless was expected. Warrington might be the most insignificent of mushroom townlets, instead of an ancient borough with a distinguished past, for all that the addresq indicates. It seems to us that the address might very properly have been drafted with some pretensions to literary and historic flavour, in preference to the stereotyped and soulless formula which has been adopted. * * *

It happens sometimes, even in England, the land of lovers of horseflesh, that the winner of many races is brought as low as to end its days between the shafts of a cab or warehouse dray, but the custom is unknown here of selling such unfortunate specimens of their race as butcher's meat at 21d. a pound. That has happened in Bavaria, where a paper displays an advertisement headed "Good Appetite ! " and with the picture of a racer, announcing that "the horse, Sad Affair,' which fell at the Baden races recently, and had to be killed, will, from Saturday next, be sold to all corners at 30 pfennige the pound." "Sad Affair " indeed! * * *

The venerable Sir Francis Sharp Powell, M.P.. who entered his 83rd year on Tuesday, was the recipient of many congratulations. Indeed, in the course of the all-night sitting, many of his friends were despatching tele- grams to the aged baronet from the Parlia- mentary Post Office. Sir Francis, whose Parliamentary experience is older thon that of any other member of the present House of 'Commons, first entered St. Stephen's as the member for Wigan in the year 1857— upwards of half-a-century ago. He sits at the present time for the same constituency, but in the interim he has represented other divisions, and, incidentally, it may be stated that he has contested more elections than any other man living. * * *

The price of beer, which is at present the subject of considerable controversy, was the cause of a great dear of trouble long before Mr. Lloyd-George became Chancellor of the Exchequer. Threepence a mart was the price fixed for beer by an Act passed in the reign of William 111., but when the duty on malt was increased in 1761, the publicans, at the instigation of the brewers, raised it to 31d. a quart. This was contrary to the law, and consquently several Westminster publicans were summoned and fined. After- wards the price was quickly lowered; but not before the consumers had forcibly sh6wn their disgust at the action of the' Trade.. Their prediction that, though the duty on malt and hope had increased, the brewers, without advancing their prices would still be able to :make great fortunes,, has been justified by history.

* * * Some of the Birkenhead Thriff Reformers had a very unhappy time of it last week. As a counterblast to Mr. Gleave's Free Trade campaign, they held several open-air meet-- ings in the borough in adv'ocacy of the Birmingham fiscal heresy, characteristically arranged after MT. Gleave had departed from the borough'. Unfortunately for them (says' the "Liverpool Post and Mercury 'I), the hecklers turned up at each meeting- in

strong force, and pelted them unmercifully with questions. Some of these questions proved such hard nuts that the speakers absolutely declined to attempt to crack them, and in one instance the gathering was so hostile that it was abandoned to the defenders of Mr. Lloyd-George's Free Trade Budget. An elderly voter, who remembered a time when it was dangerous to say a word in public against the Tory rulers of the town was heard to say, " Ah, we are getting a bit of our own back now!" * *

One of the grimmest struggles in the long history of interhational cricket is taking place at Leeds. So far, the sides are practi- cally on level terms, though the Cornstalks, with a lead of 181 and two wickets to fall look like giving England a tough proposition for the final innings to-day. By a curious eoincidence our photograph of Jessop, on page 7, and the hopeful note underneath were being printed off at the time of the most unfortunate mishap to the mighty hitter. His loss to the side cannot be estimated at the time of writing. Let us hope that the result of the match will provide sojne con- solation for unhappy England.

Death of Mr. H. G. Johnston. Many of our readers will regret to bear of the death of Mr. Henry George Johnston, who passed away at his residence in St. Mary-street on Tuesday, at the age of 56 years. For many years the deceased gentleman conducted a private school at Lorne House, Froghall-lane, and all his old pupils held him in the highest regard. The funeral took place at Grappen- ball on Thursday, the Rev. H. V. Pigot officiating. The mourners included Messrs. M. and C. Johnston (sons), Miss Johnston (daughter), Dr. Joseph, and Mr, Wel s by.

The Strike at Messrs. Whyman's. We are very pleased to be able to announce that the difficulties between Messrs. Whyman and tlfeir foundry employees have been smoothed over by the aid of tactful mediation from Alderman Smethurst. He was waited upon by a number of the men who had been locked out through the wages dispute for 17 weeks, and agreed to approach the firm. Messrs Why-man met Alderman Smethurst very readily, and his proposals were jointly agreed to. A large number of the men will return to work on Monday next, it being understood that they now accept the terms offered originally by Messrs. Whyman.

Mr. William Crosfleld's Estate. Mr. William Crosfield, a prominent member of the Liverpool City Council, and a well-known Congrega- tionalist, who died suddenly at the Town Hall after a public speech on the 17th May last, aged 71, left estate valued at £27,670 gross and at £22,006 net. Probate has been granted to his daughter, Miss D. M. Crosfield, of Fulwood-road, and his nephew, Mr. E. Crosfield Pearson, of 22, Booth-street, Manchester, solicitor. He left £2OO to his wife and daughter upon trust to dis- tribute the same at. their discretion amongst such charitable objects as they may select. The residue of his property he left to his wife for life, with remainder to his daughter, Dora Margaret Crosfield, absolutely LAWN MOWERS.—Several good machines on sale

Railway Staff Picnic. • The staff of the District Goods Office of the London and North-Western Railway at Warrington held their ginnial picnic on Saturday, when Keswick was visited. The party numbered 28 and . included Mr: Sargent ,(manager Of the Warrington District) and his staff; 1,11.: S. H. • Hunt (distriet superintendent, •Garston) ; Mr. J. B. r'rkhotiSe' (Mr. Sargent's assistant) ; and M. A- Banks- (chief clerk). Leaving Warrington at '6'.45, Keswick was reached'at 11.15 and dinner was partaken of at the. Keswick Hotel. Afterwards the party drove by' the East side of Dementwater to Rostwaite, returning: to' Keswick by the West side *_of the Lake. The drive embraced magnificent scenery and opportunity was afforded of seeing the famous Lodore Falls and the• Bowder Stone. The bowling greens in the Fitz Park afforded a pleasant change for enthusiasts in the sport. Tea was served at the Keswick Hotel at five o'clock, and the return journey was commenced at 6.5 p.m., Warrington being reached at 10 o'clock after a highly successful outing. STOCKTON HEATH'S WALKING DAY PROCESSION,

The weather conditions on Thursday were vastly different to those of last Friday, which was the proposed date of the Stockton Heath Walking Day. Crowds of people gathered on Thursday afternoon to witness the procession; composed of 1,000 Day and Sunday School scholars of St. Thomas's Church, who assembled at the Church at 4.30p.m. The Walton Band headed the procession and the route was as follows : Ellesmere-road, Whitefield-road, Cawdor-street, Orchard-street, London-road, Greenalls-avenue, and on. to a field kindly lent by Messrs. Greenall, Whitley and Co., Ltd. The chief marshal was Mr. F. Rose. Amongst those walking in the procession were the Revs. J. Collins and H. A. Lester, Messrs. T. Sharrocks, W. Mclntyre, James Whitley, A. Moss, Miss Whitlow and Miss Langley (superintendents, boys, girls and infants 'respectively). Conveyances were lent by lgessrs. G. Bowden, J. Endersby, J. Jones, Barton, J. Lillie and H. Singleton. Arrived at the field, games and sports took place. A band, under the conductorship of Mr. Wray, was in attendance on the field.

An Impudent Burg!ar.

A burglary of a Very impudent nature was com- mitted on Tuesday evening, when the house of Mr. Robert Cragg, Ellesmere-road, Lower Walton, was broken into and Is. 3d., a gentleman's coat, a quantity of food and a collar were taken. The peculiar feature about it is that Mr. and Mrs. Cragg were in bed at the time. It appears that the burglar, who is believed to be a tramp. entered through the front window and carried several coats from the hall into the back kitchen A purse containing is. 3d. was taken off the mantel- piece. The burglar then went to the pantry where he helped himself to some cheese, sucked two eggs, ate bread and jam, and consumed a pot of salmon. That was not enough, for he daubed the table and the tea- pot with jam, leaving the gas alight and the doors open as he took his leave. Mr. Cragg rose at 6 o'clock on Wednesday morning and finding the place in a state af disorder gave information to the police. The culprit has not been found.

WARRINGTON BRIDGE.

Another Meeting but Little Progress.

Another meeting of the Bridge sub-committee was held on Monday morning at the Town Hall, when Mr. Webster C.E. attended and submitted several designi for the new structure. Owing to the inconvenient hour at which the meeting was called there was a small att end/ince.

The General Purposes Committee met in the after- noon of the stme day and discussed the minutes of the previous Bridge sub-committee, re the appointment of Mr. Webster and the terms of his appointment. The meeting was of a very lengthy and acrimonious character, the question being again raised by one or two members as to the advistbility of erezting an iron bridge or a ferro-concrete one, but the former pro- posal was not entertained by the committee in general. The minute of the Bridges sub-committee was referred back to the committee for further consideration. Although Mr. Webster was in attendance, he was not called into the room and the meeting ended without any decision being arrived at.

TRAGIC OCCURRENCE AT PENKETH.

Heart Failure in A Swimming Bath.

A gloom has been cast over Penketh and Sankey by the sudden death of Fred Sparkes, aged 21. Stocks Lame, which occurred on Thursday afternoon. It appears that on the morning of Thursday deceased had a game of bowls, after which he .went home and partook of a hearty dinner. At 3 o'clock he proceeded to the Penketh Park Baths, which he had been in the habit of attending, and swam 3 or 4 yards in the water of the depth of about 5 feet. Suddenly he went down, and Daniel Atherton who was swimming near made a Plucky attempt to rescue him. Sparkes did not rise again, and on being taken out of the water artificial respiration was resorted to for an hour by Dr, Murray and P.C. Houghton. However, attempts to revive him were in vain, and it is believed that death .was due to heart failure brought about by the shock of the cold water following upon a heavy meal. The deceased who was a well-developed young fellow attended Sankey Church and was a member of the Order of Druids. Much sympathy is extended to his relatives in their tragic bereavement. The inquest is expected to take place to-day (Saturday).

BOATING DISASTER ON THE

MERSEY.

Warrington Man Drowned.

A boating fatality occurred on the Mersey between Widnes and Warrington yesterdu morning, the victim being a young man named Percy Buckley, living at 27, Atherton-street, Warrington. It appears that on Thunday evening, two friends of the deceased named Arthur Phillips, Bewsey-lane Cottage, near Warring- ton, and John Badger, 101, Bramhall-street, Warring- ton, set out in a sailing boat for Widnes, where they had arranged to meet the deceased and William Mason 176, Wellfield-street. They had borrowed a small punt for the purpose of going ashore. When they reached Houghton Marsh Point, near. Widnes, the vessel ran. aground. They remained on her over-night and yesterday morning they went ashore in the punt, and walked to. Widnes where they Met the deceas,ed' and Mason. They retraced thek steps. to Houghtmi Marsh Point, and were going back tO the sailing boat, but when they got in mid-stre,ant the punt sprang a leak and in a few minutes it filled and sank.. Badger' swam ashore and his three companions clung' to .the up-turned punt whiclvdrifted up the river at a terrible pace, as the tide was coming in.. They remained in this perilous position for about half an hour when ,Bnekley became exhausted, relinguished his hold. and was drowned. After drifting a long way., hirfri.ends. were at length rescued by Samuel Rustage, of Cartwright-street and Harry Brown, coal dealer, Warrington, who were boating on the river. The body of Buckley has not yet been recovered.

NEXT WEEK'S PROGRAMME.

Next week Mr. Sam Livesey, who is a great favourite with Warrington theatre goo's, will present a strong company in the following repertoire :—Monday, Friday and Saturday, "The King of Crime " •, Tuesday and Wednesday, " Lost by Drink " ; Thursday, "Called back." Mr. Livesey, who has gained a high reputation by his portrayal ,of the " Village Blacksmith" will appear in several interesting character studies and the company will also include Miss Maggie Edwards,who has previously played here with considerable success.

Mr. Tom Smith on Hides.

Sir,—Mr. Tom Smith, of Burnley, cannot be con- gratulated upon his second open air speech in favour of Tariff Reform. The last time he spoke I had occasion to point out a glaring false statement in respect to all Anglo-American hides in 1005-6 going back to the States. He has not replied to this. He is unable to substantiate his statement and has not the candour to acknowledge he was wrong. But in the first address if the arguments were misleading,because based on false statements, what must we say of his Ilast effort?

In the report of his adaress I find the following false statements :

First.—" The hides they raised in this country, altogether in themselves were insufficient for the requirements of this country. Yet they exported more hides than they imported." Taking the figures for the last five years the imports, after deducting the re-exports, were £13,094,413, while the exports were £2,870,542, showing we import for home consumption five times as great as we export. Second.—" To-day New York controlled the Hide Market of the world."

This is entirely wrong, as New York has very little influence on the hide market. If he had said that the Chicago Packers by the help of the American 15 per cent. duty on hides controlled the hide market of America Mr. Tom Smith, of Burnley, would have been surprised to find himself nearer the truth than he usually gets.

mid:—" The United States took the opportunity of liquidating the adverse balance between the two countries, and we paid her la*rgely in gold, specie, and silver."

For the four years ending 1908 America sent us in gold and silver £10,000,000 more than we sent her. It is difficult to know how to deal with Mr. Smith, he seems to stand on his head and see everything upside down. Instead of our liquidating the adverse balance by paying America in coin or bullion, as he seems to think, the facts as stated show that America sends us more than we send her.

Fourth.—" He, Mr. Smith, would mention the fact that last year £90,000,000 came to this country in the shape of interest on investments abroad. And "Not one penny of that £90,000,000 paid income tax."

First his figure of £90,000,000 is altogether inaccurate and shows that Mr. Tom Smith of Burnley's knowledge is far from being up-to-date. His statement that this does not pay income tax is altogether false. This income is taxed under Schedules C. and D the same as other incomes.

• Fifth.—" If the United States was the principal source of supply for this country's beef, then it naturally followed that the hides which came off beef found their way back to the States. They then found employment in wages in the tanning of the hides." "He, Mr. Smith made the statement, that the United States sent this country their beef ant then took the hides back again to the manufacturer and that this country paid through the nose for the finished- article."

The United States do mit takekback one tenth of the hides ,they send us on live cattle. The leather they send us in bulk is not made from these hides, but front a different class altogether. "Paying through the nose" for this leather! I suppose by this he means we pay dearly for the leather although why he does not say so in intelligent English Ido not know. The fact is we buy American leather cheaper than the American manufacturers and this ie the great griev- ance of the leather trade, tanners, and boot manufac- turers in America at the present time. They point out that the leather trade of England gets the advantage of their cheap leather, because by exporting the American tanner recovers 99 per cent of the duty which he had to pay when the hides were imported. Sixth.—" The United States admitted these hides free of duty." This statement is also fal3e. An attempt was made to get these hides in duty free as" returned empties" but was unsuccessful. Any Binkenhead hides going back to the States pay a duty of 15 per cent., The existence of this duty emphasizes a point I have elsewhere raised that you cannot in justice have duties to protect one form of industry and leave others unprotected. The cattle-raising industry of America has as much right to protection as the tanning industry, but the American tanners now realize that having to pay 15 per cent. tax on their raw material the advantage arising from their share of the general plunder becomes worthless. I have dealt dbove merely with the false statements. The' false inferences from the false statements might occupy another letter, but let this suffice. I can now leave Mr. Tom Smith to explain his false statements— if be can. I think that we have some right to com- plain of the members of the Warrington Tariff Reform League for 'bringing, and rebringing, a man to advocate their cause in the town who so persistently falsifies the facts. It . may be freely admitted that there is room for differences of opinion in regard to the inferences and the arguments in this large and com- plex question, but surely it would be possible to bring to the town speakers who are prepared to give accurately the facts so that we might at once try to get to close quarters upon the deductions derived .from them.

CHARLES E. PARKER. Penlteth. June 30th, 1909.

MARRIED WOMEN TEACHERS.

Pending Application.

Mr. Justice Eve gave leave yesterday to issue short notice of an application which he will hear in the High Court on Friday next, for an interim injunc- tion to restrain the Warrington Education Committee from acting upon the notices • given to two married lady teachers (Mrs. Stansfield and Mrs. Grounds) pending the trial of the action for a permanent injunction.

Seven persons have been poisoned at Barry through eating shellfish picked from the quay walls of the dock at that place, and one of them, a schoolgirl, Jane Tobin, twelve years of age, died.

SMART PUNISHMENT FOR A RAILWAY THIEF. —A MIDNIGHT CAPTURE. The Warrington Police Court presented the appear- ance of a 'warehouse on Tuesday. for on the solicitors' and reporters' desks were piled a great assortment of articles, including boots, clothing, gloves, dress material electro-plated goods, ornaments, soap, biscuits, sardines, and a bicycle, all the proceeds of robberies from the London and North-Western Railway goods warehouse. Bank Quay. A man named Herbert Rose, of 18, Cumberland-street, Latchford, was charged on remand with breaking and entering the warehouse on Sunday. June 20th, and ste3ling the pairs of boots, valued at 355. ; also with stealing between Monday, May 31st, and Tuesday, June Ist, from Bank Quay Goods Warehouse a waterproof shooting coat, valued at three guineas; also with stealing from the warehouse between May 21th and 28th two rolls of satin cloth valued at £5 Is, 2d. ; and also with stealing between February 22nd and 24th, 1908, from the goods yard of the Cheshire Lines Com- mittee a gent's bicycle, valued at a Bs. He pleaded guilty to all the charges. The magistrates on the bench were Mr. H. Roberts (in the chair), Alderman Bolton, and Messrs. J. Rymer Young, W. Richardson, E. Woods, C. Jackson Holmes and I', Locker. Mr. IL T. Tait, solicitor, London, who prosecuted on behalf of the railway companyoaid that in consequence of frequent and somewhat mysterious robberies at the goods warehouse,a special watch was kept by Detective Clarke and Police-constable Hewitt, of the company's police. On Saturday night, June 19th, they were secreted in the building. Shortly after midnight they heard a noise in the warehouse resembling the unpack- ing of a case. On proceeding cautiously to the spot they found the prisoner crawling away on his hands and knees. When caught by the officers, prisoner said "It is a fair cop. I will give in." It appeared the prisoner had gained an entrance through one of the windows on the ground floor of the goods office. In carrying out his work the prisoner generally opened a packing case, took out the goods and then fastened the case securely. When the officers searched the prisoner's house a number of articles were found which the prisoner had admitted having stolen. At least a dozen robberies could be traced to the prisoner extending over a considerable period. Some of the cloth goods had been sold by his relations to people in the neighbourhood where he resided on the representation that they were only patterns. In respect to the theft of the bicycle, Mr. C. E. Pinfold, solicitor for the Cheshire Lines Railway, said two bicycles were transhipped at Ancoats, Mauchester, and arrived at the goods warehouse at Warriugton. Later one was missed, and was subsequently found in the prisoner's house. The magistrates sent prisoner to gaol for twelve months. RAILWAY COMPANY AND STREET PAVING— CORPORATION WIN THEIR CASE.

An important case was heard at a special court held] at the Warrington Police Court on Tuesday to decide the claim of the Cheshire Lines Committee to be exempt under Section 22 of the Private Street Works Act of 1892 from the payment of an apportionment for paving of a passage between 2 to 72, Elaine-street, and 2to 80, Hulme-street. Councillor H. Roberts presided, and other magistrates present were :—Messrs. W. Richardson, 'l'. Locker, J. Rymer Young,and C. Jackson Holmes.—Mr. Rigby Swift (instructed by Mr. J. Lyon Whittle, Town Clerk), represented the Corporation, and Dr. Atkinson (instructed by Messrs. Lingard and Hamp) appeared on behalf of the Cheshire Lines Committee.— Dr. Atkinson pointed out that the section under which the committee claimed exemption, laid it down that a railway company should not be deemed owner or occupier for the purposes of the Act in respect of land "upon which any street shall wholly or partially front or abut and which shall at the time of laying out of such ' street be used by such company solely as part of their line of railway, canal, siding, station or works arid shall have no direct com- munication with such street." They submitted that those conditions obtained in the present case. There was no suggestion that the Cheshire Lines Com- mittee had used the land in Hume-street for other than railway purposes. That land had absolutely been put to no use whatever except as a precautionary strip of land. At one time a stable was erected, but no rent was paid for it. With regard to the land in Elaine- street, the only use that had been made of it, was that some of the platelayers in the employ of the railway company had small gardens. Those men had used the land without permission, and bad paid no rent what- ever. Certain small gaps were made in order that the men could get on the land, but those openings bad been closed up by March ISth.—Evidence in support of the committee's claim was given by Mr. Charles Henry Atkins, assistant surveyor and engineer, who stated that so far as he knew the committee had no intention of using the land for any other than railway purposes. The paving of the streets would not benefit them in any way.—George Monks, permanent way inspector, stated that the gardens had been there for ten or twelve years. The gates were made out of the fence which had been erected by the company and were kept locked.— Mr. Rigby Swift, addressing the Bench for the Corpor- ation, said that if the railway company were exempted the whole of the cost must be borne by the other ownels of property in that street. Before the claim cocld succeed it was necessary that the strip of land should be used for purposes of the station, siding, or works, and the mere fact that the Lind was acquired and was not used at all did not give the company the privilege of being free from the obligations of other property owners. The section was very explicit, and the fact that now and again there was some tipping done there was not sufficient to bring the land under the descrip- tion of "works." Tbe specific duty of user was implied in the section. -He also contended that there was a communication between the land and the street, and said he could also bring evidence to show that the gaps in the fence in Elaine-street were not closed up until March 18th.—After the Bench had con- sulted in private, the Chairman said the railway com- pany had not proved to the satisfaction of the Bench that the laud was being used solely as part of the rail- way, station, siding, or works, or that there was no communication with the street. They therefore dis- missed the objection, and on the application of Mr. Rigby Swift they allowed 20 guineas costs.—Mr. Rigby Swift said that there waa £450 actually in dispute in the case.
