Warrington Borough Court.

SATURDAY.—Before Messrs. J. W. Hayward (presiding), R. T. Fairclough, A. J. Jolley, Councillor Dr. Joseph Dr. Markel, Mr. John Upson, and Mr. T. Pemberton. SHOP THEFT.—James Hampsen, no settled abode, was charged on remand with having stolen three pairs stockings from the shop of Richard Tebbutt, 115, Bridge-street, on Monday.—The Chief Constable said that prisoner was before the Court on the previous Tuesday. Impressions of his finger prints had been sent to Scotland Yard, and it was found that he had been twice previously convicted for theft.—Prisoner, who had nothing to say, was sent to gtol for a month with hard labour.

MONDAY.—Before the Mayor (Alderman W. J. Forshaw), the Deputy Mayor (Alderman James Smethurst), Alderman James Evans, Councillor Wilkinson, Messrs. H. Thornton, G. Pilling and William Furness. ASSAULT ON HIS MOTHER.—Percy Quinn, Do!menu's- lane, was charged with being drunk and disorderly on Saturday evening in Dolman's-lane, and also with assaulting his mother by kicking her.—lnspector Forster said that prisoner was drunk and he took two running kicks at his mother whilst she was on the floor. Mrs. Quinn said that although he had kicked her, it was not she whom he tried to kick. It was her other son that he intended to kick.—Sergeant Barnett said that the prisoner made a deliberate kick at her. Prisoner: I deny it; I made a kick at my brother.— Sergeant Barnett: His brother was not. there, and had not been for a couple of minutes.—Prisoner maintained that he kicked his mother unintentionally; he always took his mother's part.—The bench imposed a fine of t.)os. and costs or one month's imprisonment. TRADESPEOPLE SUMMONED FOR OBSTRUCTION.— John Cheadle, greengrocer, GG, Orford lane, was sum- moned for causing obstruction in Orford-lane on the 19th June, by leaving various goods on the footpath.— P.C. Gibson gave evidence and said that he told defendant's wife to remove the goods early in the afternoon, and later he went round and they were still there.—Defendant said that he had only just go e in when the policeman came round again.—The case was dismissed.—Elizaheth Smith, greengrocer, 86, Orford. lane was summoned for obstructing .the footpath on the 19th June by leaving various goods there.—P.C. Gibson said that defendant said she was not going to take them in Until Cheadle took his in.—The case was dismissed.—Joseph Marsh, Ironmonger, 15, Padaate- lane, was summoned for obstruction on the 19th June. —Defendant pleaded not guilty.—P.C. Gibson said that on the date in question he saw some step-ladders and fireguards and other things. He asked the defendant's dbughter to remove them. On going later he found that some of the things had been taken in. He went into the shop and saw the defendant,who said that there were several tradesmen about the town who left their goods on the footpath. He was not going to take them in, the constable could report him. Defendant brought several more things out and placed them with the ot hers. Defendant (to constable) : How many feet of the parapet are claimed by the Corporation ?—Constable: I don't know.—Defendant: Do you know how many feet are owned by the landlords ?—Constable: No.— Defendant: You are evidently not very well educated in these details. When you came to my place you found my daughter at five minutes to three. She complied to your request as to the taking in of the gaods with the exception of taking two fireguards in. About five o'clock I entered the shop; I had no sooner entered than 'you came in. In what tone did you speak to my daughter ?—Constable: In a respectable manner.— Defendant: You addressed her in a most indignant manner and said that you would force her to take them

in.—Constable • Nothing of the sort took place.— Defendant, addressing the Bench, said that, opposite his place of business was another shop, in the same line of business. His parapet was only 4ft. fins. That person bad at the same time three dolly tubs out. When the constable told him (the defendant) that he was exercis- ing his duty by telling him to take the goods in, be asked the constsble if he had been to the other people across the road. He said be bad not. That was the way he was carrying out the law. The width of his parapet was lift. 2 inches. Eight feet was claimed by the landlord. He intended to mark it, "private."—The Chief Constable said that the constable was quite flabbergasted when he spoke to the defendant., as the magistrates would see from the way in which he had spoken in the Court that morning. 'the defendant did not use gentle words to the constable. The defendant was summoned 25 years ago.—A fine of ss. and costs was imposed. DOGS. COLLARS, AND LICENCES.—John Wiflism Clare, 11, Stevenson-street, was tined is. for keeping a dog without a collar.—Emlyn Evans, 177, Thelwill-lane, was summoned for having a dog which bad a collar without a name upon it.—The defendant said the plate had been taken off .—The case was withdrawn.—Herbert Hughes,farmer, was lined ls.for keeping a dog without a collar John Himsworth,general dealer, 86,Buttermarket- street, was summoned for keeping a dog without the name on the collar.—Defendant said that the plate had been taken off and another was on order.—The case was withdrawm—Alexander McKenzie, 36, Winwick-road, was.summoned for keeping a dog with a collar, but no address on it.—Defendant said the collar had the name on, but it was the name of the husiness. He thought that was sufficient.—A flue of Is. was imposed.—For keeping a dog without a licence.—Stephen Albert Turner, 7, Kerfoot-street, was fined Bs. and costs.

TCESDAY.—Before Alderman Boltt.n (in the chair) ail Mr. Edwin Woods.

FACTORY PROSECITTIONS.—The Bishops Wharf Carrying Company, Limited, were summoned under the Factory Act for failing to enter into a register a test certificate relating to the use of a sling chain. Mr. John Herbert Warren, inspector of factories, proved the case and a fine of 10s. and costs was imposed.—The English Products Company, Longford, Warrington, were fined 10s. and costs tor having certain machinery at their works insecurely fenced.

THURSDAY.—Before Mr. C. White (chairman), Alder- man Evans. and Messrs. AV. Roberts, J. Rymer Young, Dr. Naden and W. T. Williams. A MEAN THEFT.—Thomas Humphries, Priestley- street, uas charged with stealing a spirit level valued at 4s. Gd., the property of William hash, a joiner—The Chief Constable stated that the prisoner and prosecutor were working together on the renovation of St. Alban's Church, Bewses-street. On Monday last the prosecutor lent the level to the foreman brickla3er. Subsequently it was missed, and front inquiries made it was ascer- tained that the defendant bad pawned it at Mr. Houghton's pawnshop for 9d.—The defendant was fined ss. and costs, or 7 days' imprisonment in default.

NEWSBOYS' GIIIEYANCES.—WiIIiaM Worsley, a news- boy, was charged with assaulting and beating John Brookes on the 29th June.—Complainant stated that he was on his newspaper round in Hnlme-street and met defendant who had served one of his (complainant's) customers. When complainant spoke to him about it, defendant struck him a blow in the mouth.—The magis- trate ordered defendant to be imprisoned for one day. DOMESTIC TROLJBLES.—Joseph Hoyles, of 20,11aydock- street, was summ.:.ned for persistent cruelty, thereby causing his wife (Martha) of 19, Turner-street, to leave him.—Complainant stated that they bad only been tharried four months, and in that short space of time he had not given her_ a grain of comfort. Whenever she visited her mother he generally thrashed her. He bad told her that she was a dying ghost."—The case was adjourned for a month, the Police Court Missionary (Mr. Firth) promising to do all he could to bring about an amicable settlement.

Wycliffe P.S.A.

Tbere was a good meeting on Sunday, presided over by Mr. W. Beswick, who spoke upon "The power of man in Christ." All man's power, hopes and aspirations were from God, and in Clod. Mr. W. W. Drewitt gave the address upon "Woe is me,that f sojourn in Mesecb, that I dwell in the tents of Kedar." He drew several lessons from these places of sin, arid wicked persons, but pointed out that many of God's faithful ones dwelt amongst them and acted as leaven. So in the experi- ence of .each one of us daily, we were amongst evil, men of unclean lips, some even as whited sepulchre., full of foulness and lustful passions. God would honour us and bless us and make us a blessing, even in places like these, if we faithfully served Him, trusting in the Almighty One to help and deliver.—One new member received a very hearty welcome. Mr. Payton presided at the organ and Mr. G. Smiley and Mr. R. Mills also took part in the meeting. The meeting closed with the singing of "Work for the night is coming," which was very heartily joined in by the brethren.

Opening of the Warrington Church House

The Bishop of Liverpool visited Warrington on Monday afternoon to open the new Warrington Church house, which is situated in Bridge-street (over • Mr. T. J. Lee's shop.) The occasion was one of great interest to Churchpeople, for the need of such a centre of Church life and work in the town his long been felt. There are two rooms, one being capable of accommodating a meeting of from fifty to one hundred people, while the other is furnished as a writing and sitting-room. The nucleus of a library has been forthcoming, and there will he a good supply of current periodicals. The sub- scription for the first year has been fixed at a minimum of half a guinea, and the wives of members will be admitted to the use of the rooms free of charge. Much of the furniture has been presented by friends. At the opening ceremony, the Mayor of Warrington presided over a large assembly which included the Right Rev. the Lord Bishop of Liverpool (Dr. Ct.a vasse), the Rev. Canon Penrhyn, Mr. W. Peter Rylands and Mr. John White (three of the vice-presidents), the Rev. Canon Willis (chairman of the committee), Mr. J. McGregor (hon. treasurer), the Rev. H. A. Lester (hon. secretary). the Rev. G. W. Dickinson, Mr. T. J. Lee and Mr. H. Beresford Melville (members of the com- mittee of management), the Rev. E. C. E. Carleton, the Rev. W. Bracecarnp, ,Mrs. Robert Pierpoint and Mr. Herbert Woods.

The MAYOR said he entirely agreed with the objects of the Church House, and he was sure that all good Cburchpeople and other Christian people would agree with them.

The BISHOP of LTVERPOOL, expressing his pleasure at being present, said that on all sides he saw that Warrington was moving. They had built a new street and made it broad and baAdsome, and a street which would do credit to any town in South-west Lancashire. Not only. was the town moving, but he trusted that the Church was moving. Not long since he had the privilege of being present at the opening of Orford Church. A few Sundays ago—Whit Sunday—he was present at Holy Trinity Church, and he saw the new vestry which had been built and the new exit from the church, which would be for the safety of the worshippers, and that afternoon be had been asked to open the new Church House. He was justified in saying that the Church, as well as the muni- cipality of Warrington, was moving. (Applause). Referring to the usefulness of the Church House, the Bishop said that it would help to unify the Church in that part ot the diocese. It would prove a centre to which the clergy and laity could resort. The great danger in the Church of England was of Churchpeople 'becoming detitched unite._ and forgetting that they belonged to a great historic Church. A great danger to a clergyman, whether he was in the town or country, was of becoming parochial, and limiting his sym- pathies and his work to his diocese, and forgetting that be was ordained to be, not a priest merely of the parish in which he worked, but a priest of the Church of God. Such a house as that would help to bring the clergy and laity together to realise their oneness. More than that,he believed it would prove to be a literary centre. It was most important that the clergy should be up to date with regard to their reading, and when clergymen were isolated, as they were apt to become, their reading was also apt to become rusty, if they would allow him to use a University term. A good library, not only in a cathedral city but in a Church house, was a wonderful incentive to read. Many of the clergy wanted to read, but they could not afford out of their limited incomes to buy the books they wanted, and if someday they could add to the library of the Church House a circulating library it would help to educate the clergy and laity in a very forcible manner indeed. (Herr, hear). At the present moment there was a deeper interest in Church matters than there had been for years, a deeper desire to know the history of the Church of Eugland—a history he ventured to remark, which, with all their short comings, they had no need to be ashamed of. (Applause). Be heartily wished them God-speed, and as Bishop of the diocese he was thankful that the promoters of the Church House had started on such excellent lines. Mr. JOHN Wnrre then addressed the meeting ou the objects of the Church House and read a list of dona- tions, amounting to £6O. He referred to the aid War- rington Church Institute, started flfty-one years ago by the Rev. Horace Powys, Rector of Warrington (subse- quently Bishop of Sodor and Man), and said it perished through the spirit of parochialism. The RECTOR, in proposing a. vote of thanks to the Mayor for presiding, alluded to the union between Church and State. There had been, he said, a union between Church and State for more than 1,000 year in this country, and they were glad to be able to think that the Church existed more than 600 years before that. There were those who sometimes felt the connection between Church and State a little irksome. Doubtless there were occasions when the connection had been a little irritating and almost exasperating, but be was sure they would be foolish to try to *mote a divorce between Church and State when the connection had on the whole been very beneficial to both.

The Rev. Canon STEVENSON seconded, and the vote was he,,rtily passed. The Bishop was thanked on the proposition of the Rev. Canon PENRHYN seconded by Mr. W. PATER. itYLANDS.

In responding to the vote of thanks, his LORDSHIP said the cathedrals in the past days owed their origin mainly to ecclesiastics. The cathedial of the twentieth century (Liverpool) owed its existence almost entirely to the laymen of the diocese. It was hoped that the laity would realise their position and respon- sibility, and take their proper place as guardians and champions of the Church. He hoped that clerical and episcopal jealousy would never for one moment damp their ardour in the diocese of Liverpool. The Rev. 11. A. LESTER acknowledged numerous gifts which had been made to the Church House. Further help in that direction was still required, and from £BO to £lOO a year would be needed to keep the Church House going.

MR. WILLIAM LEACH OF HATTON.

I was born at Hatton about four miles from Wan ington, on the 6th of August, 1839, in which place I have resided ever since. I received my education as a lad in two neigh- bouring schools, namely, at Stretton under Mr. John Warburton, and Daresbury, under Mr. Isaac Walton ' (not of angling fame). I finished my schooling at 13 years of age and started work as an apprentice under my ' father, Thomas Leach, who was a boot and shoemaker. It was in the old thatched cottage, recently pulled down and formerly known as Hatton Old Hall, where we, together with my brother John, laboured. Not a few men of Methodist fame have sheltered under the roof of the old cottage. It may interest some of your readers if I mention but a few. • The Rev. Samuel Wilkinson, a Wesleyan minister, has spent many a happy hour with us, and lei it be to the credit of Hatton in having sent forth so noble a character, for Samuel was born in the , village. Shall I refresh the memories of some • when I add the names of Rev. George Dickenson, Dr. William Autcliff (of Primitive -Methodist fame), Dr. Agar Beet, who by the way is still labouririg on, and many others I could mention. There is, however, another name so dear to me that I must make mention of it—a recent Presi- dent of the Primitive Methodist Connexion in the person of Rev. Thomas Hankey Hunt. He was also born in Hatton, and was an old school-fellow of mine. I have many times wondered if he remembered the time when he used to stone the birds, and if, while in his now worthy calling, he is fully repentant for the school-day sins of "Tom Hunt." I have vivid recollections of being in the very pleasant company of Miss Eliza Weaver Bradburn, who was paying a short visit to my home. She will be better known by some older Wesleyans as the daughter of the Rev. Samuel Bradburn,who rose from the cobbler's bench tol the Presidential chair of the Wesleyan Methodist Conference. Miss Brad- burn was a poetess of no mean order, and her work always gave expression to those finer qualities inherited from her noble father. No doubt my own father will still be remembered by many. He laboured for more than 60 years as a Wesleyan local preacher and died at the ripe old age of 82, after having been rejected for the ministry on account of the fact that medical evidence considered that he would go into a consumption. He has transmitted his physical powers to myself, now, 70 years of age,to my brother Thomas Leach of Stockton Heath (late of Runcorn) and my bother John Leach who died two years ago in his 77th year. One wonders how it is that even doctors can be so deceived. He always attributed his good health and longevity to frugal habits and total abstinence, a rule of life which I have rigidly followed. Might Ibe pardoned if I pay a tribute to the memory of my dead brother John, who was considered to be one of the finest self-taught mathematicians for many a mile round. He had the very great honour of being introduced by correspond- ence to the late Mr. Gladstone. He compli- mented my brother upon his work, and as a re'cognition of his abilities presented him with several astronomical instruments. He also endeavoured to obtain a post for him in Greenwich Observatory, but as this was at a period when late on in life he did not succeed.

At 34 years of age I relinquished the following of my trade and, started an agency for the Prudential, Assurance Com- pany, remaining in their service up to my retirement on a pension last year. I have calculated as near as possible that in my ordinary duties for them I have walked 100,000 miles. These figures are rather under than over the mark for I have not added extra and special journeys. This distance I suppose is equal to about four times the dis- tance round the world. I gave up in my 35th year of service and felt that my rest had been well-earned. I cannot speak too highly of the treatment the Company have always meted out to me. I have watched with keen interest the growth of this great industrial concern from its few millious of capital to its now enormous dimensions and capital of over 72 millions. It is needless for one to say that during my experience as an agent I have seen many interesting sides of life. I could tell some humorous stories as a result but time and space, at this juncture, will not allow. Politics have played a r,ather small part in my life as party changes have been but few in this wide-spread division of Knutsford. However I am disagreeably. reminded of the old "Protection Days" and have need to be thankful that at least I have lived sufficiently long to see the great and manifold blessing of Free Trade conferred upon the inhabitants of Great Britain. I like sugar in my tea but not at the price I paid in 1860 of sd. per lb. I well remember in those days bread at B.ld. per four pound loaf, coal at is. 6d. per cwt., flour at 30s. per load and common tea at 4s. per lb., and other neces- saries in proportion. No, dear reader, the question of Free Trade v. Protection is not a question of politics with me. But rather the choosing of a principle which carries with it the very life's blood of the people. I have

observed with entire satisfaction the gradual elevation of the poorer classes as a result of freedom and liberty in commerce, whether it be Agricultural or Industrial. I have seen the average wage of the farm labourer rise from 12s. per week to about 18s. per week and other departments of labour benefit in comparison. I have noticed the red "neck scarf" of the farm labourer's boy superseded by a cleat), linen collar. I have noticed also that the more responsible the labourer has been made by freedom and better pay, that his moral tone has developed accordingly. I have seen my own mother stop a drunken riot more than once, and I am glad to say that the rural dwellers of this part at least are decidedly far more temperate than 30 years ago. Perhaps I might take credit for a little musical advancement in mylwn village, for I well remember introducing instru- mental music in the old Wesleyan Chapel. I taught several young men to play the violin and later on I introduced an American organ and so assisted the old-time vocal choirs to render even better service. I was always prompted by the feeling that by placing a higher ideal before people you lessened the temptation to do things immoral. Violins were expensive things to buy in those days, especially when I remind you that my average wage was about 12s. to 14s. per week, with a wife and family to keep. So I made two with the aid of my shoemaker's tools. My early struggles could never have been combated without the aid of my good wife who I am thankful to say is still alive. I ask your readers to judge whether I have done my country due service or not when I tell them that my family numbers 12, ten of whom are living. The advancement in agriculture during my time has been very marked indeed, a greater quantity of better class produce now being obtained. The winter's music of the " flail " has given place to the smoke and hum-drum of the thrashing machine. The " sickle " has been superseded by the self- binding (reaper, and the old-fashioned wooden plough is no longer in use. Yet after all there was some charm in the old methods. It was delightful to watch the chaff listlessly flying round the cosy barn as each measured stroke of the " flail " separated it from the corn. And although the toiling of the reaper was somewhat wearisome, harvest-time brought with it a more real picture of nature than the mechanical methods now general. The healthy competition of the old-time ploughing match seemed to bring out the best that was in the mind and body of the rural teamster.

I cannot say very much as to the internal workings of the town of Warrington. But I have watched with interest its steady growth as an industrial centre. One thing I have noticed is the pollution of the once beautiful river Mersey. It is a long time now since I sat and watched the salmon leaping in the Latchford Weir. I suppose the growth of industries has added to their destruction, and if it has meant industrial advancement then we must be satisfied that by the sacrifice of the fresh-water salmon we have gained either a wire mill or a soap works. One of my sons once heard a visitor to Warrington say.: that he never was in a more convenient town,for if you got drunk on Warrington ale and fell down and tore your clothing you could fasten it again with a Warrington pin. If your face was blackened with smoke from Dallam Forge you could wash it clean with Crosfield's Warrington Soap. So if we have lost the sport of salmon fishing we have gained increased prosperity. In conclusion might I add that my life until now has been quietly lived, and I would not have consented to send you these few lines for any other motive than that my sole desire is to say to our younger men that whatever their position in life may be, however humble their lot, they can at least do something to assist the unceasing movement for moral progress, political freedom, and social reform.

GRAIN ELEVATORS AND FLOUR MILLS IN WESTERN CANADA

The Departrcent of the Interior has recently issued a map of that pint of Canada lying West of the Great Lakes, showing the positions of grain elevators and Flour and oatmeal milts together with tables showing their individual and .collective capacity. A few copies are obtainable from tbe Canadian Government Office, it and 12, Charing Cross, S.W. In the three provinces of Manitoba, Alberta, and Saskatchewan there are 650 grain elevators, with an aggregate capacity of 42,868,900 bushels. The terminal elevators at Lake Superior have a capacity of 14,826,00. Thus the total elevator capacity of Western Canada amounts to 78,016,140 bushels. Manitoba has fifty-flour and oatmeal mills with a daily capacity of 17,365 barrels ,• Saskatchewan has twenty-five mills with a. possible daily output of 8,365 barrels; while Alberie has seventeen mills with a possible daily output of 1,530 barrel,

jimeWBENGTON

THE OLD BLUEBACKS.

Warrington's First Volunteers.

We reminded our readers a few weeks ago that the month of June brought the Jubilee of the Warrington. Volunteers; but the meeting of 1859 was far from marking the beginning of the Volunteer movement in Warrington. TO go back to beginnings is to find ourselves in stirring days when the air was electric and feeling ran high. It was the spring of 1798 and there were real causes for the quickened pulse of the English people. The French had mustered an army 75,000 strong on the sands of Boulogne and had arrogantly given it the title The Army of England." Bonaparte, fresh from jiis victories in Italy, was at the head, and his name was already being hissed with hatred wherever the English people gathered. And home troubles gathered thickly. The insurrection in Ireland was on the point of breaking forth and England was disturbed by plots and rumours of rebellion. In response to what was felt to be the need of the hour men of every rank rallied for action. Soldiers were made in an hour. With a uniform without and a fine scorn for " Bony " within, they braced themselves for action. So widespread was the movement that Bonaparte confessed that it had thwarted his plans. The invasion of England was not going to be a decisive battle on the coast,.but a war against an armed nation, and for such a task, if he had the courage, he certainly had,not the resources. Warrington narrowly missed the honour, which fell to Lancaster, of being the first

JAMES ASHTON, alias "DEATH OR GLORY." ' (From Ix Photograph by Walter Crompion,of the Oil Painting in, the Warrington Museum and Art Gallery.)

provincial town to raise a corps, but though second information we imagine it was easily first in the fine and varied qualities of its men, and it had • the additional honour of never having cost its King or country a single shilling for arms, pay or accoutre- ment. We have not been able to discover that any appeal was. made or that any one individual gave shape to the movement. With a common impulse the able-bodied men of the town, clergy, doctors, lawyers, shop- keepers, took their places and the Volunteer corps was a reality. At the first muster 160 took their places and twenty mpre joined later. We will not trouble our readers with their names, but may mention that the com- mand was given to-Edward Dakin; Esq., and that Thomas Pemberton, James Nicholson and James Leigh, Esqrs., were captains. The lieutenants were Thomas Skitt, Edward Greenall and Peter Dalton, Esqrs. The funds were raised by public sub- scription and never fell below the needs of the corps. Zeal for the cause broke out in unexpected places. Workingmen denied themselves of luxuries and the ladies of articles of dress. Most remarkable of all was an outburst of enthusiasm on the part of the minister of Hill Cliff Baptist Chapel. This peaceful and amiable man, John Thompson. refused to be supported by his congregation and eked out a living by keeping 'a school, but out of his scanty earnings he gave liberally to the fund and publicly urged his congregation todo the same. " Brethren " he said on one 'occasion, to 'clinch a special appeal, "I had laid aside five pounds to buy a new suit of clothes, but these I have shall last me another year. I will give the money to the Volunteers, and I expect you too will give liberally, for I am now coming out of the pulpit, and will hold the plate at the ' door myself." Such an example was more eloquent., than words and the collection exceeded twenty pounds.

that there would be an outbreak of nicknames among the men. The technical names for the three companies into which the corps was divided were Grenadier, Centre and Light company, but these names were too tame for the mischievous wits, and the Grenadiers became the" Heavenly" company from their tat! stature, we may suppose, the second became the " Maltouts " from its members firing so badly, and the 3rd the "Roast Beef" company from good appetites that dwelt there.

The second parade ground was a "field at Cockhedge, but eventually a permanent meeting place was fixed upon at Harts-Head meadow the ground now occupied by Arpley Station. •A poet thus describes their advent at six o'clock on the mornings of the parade: In buskins gemmed with dew, HiS soul-inspiring call the bugler blew ; Drummers and corporals were seen, Starting from forth our alleys green. - The pipe-clayed ,Volunteer rejoiced to hear, And six-foot tailors grasped the sergeant's spear.

These early moiming drills were not the tame meetings we might imagine. England's nerves were stretched with a sense of danger and the '• doul-inspiring call" of the bugler roused many besides the volunteer heroes. Theit wives, children and sweethearts were there, and the elite of Warrington—the Egertons, the Pattens, the Herons, the Parrs, the Bowers and many others—gave the encouragement of their presence. But space does not allow a complete sketch of the "death or glory" boys. Of one or tvto outstanding events in their history and of some of the relics a the regiment we promise to tell in another chapter. A.M. .

THE UNIFORM.

A large portion of the money raised went to the purchase of fine uniform. • The style adopted was the old " Windsor " blue trimmed with white and with scarlet collar and facings, the gilt buttons/bearing a crown and the letters L.W.V. (Loyal Warrington Volunteers.) The waistcoat and pantaloons wete white, and the head cover was the round hat of the period covered with an enormous bear-skin cover. This hat was adorned with a cockade and a white feather tipped with red. The predominance of the blue colour led some one to call them by the familiar name of Blueback (the local name for the Fieldfare) and' Bluebacks " henceforward they were. When in 1803 the second crop of Volunteers adopted the scarlet coat the Bluebacks "-retaliated by dubbing them "Robin Redbreasts," and as " Bluebacks " and " Red Breasts "they will always remain in local history.

===== 0002601_19090703_art0076.txt | included=true | included =====
"DEATH OR GLORY BOYS."

'7ln the first test that was put upon their courage the " Blips " won golden opinions. Before any public parade was ventured upon they met for drill at six in the morning and on three days a week in the old assembly room in Golden-square. At one of these early drills a letter was read from the Lord Lieutenant of the County which was the occasion of a stirring and memorable scene. The Government had received private and reliable information that the Irish insurrection was ripe and the day of rising fixed upon It was reported that France was ready to invade, and that the emissaries of the Irish rebellion in England had made preparations for combat. The volunteers had only enlisted for the protection of their native town, but the letter asked whether

under the critical circumstances they would extend their protection to the County for five miles round Warrington. It was decided to consult all the private members of the corps. The Commander drew a chalked line across the *floor of the ballroom and when the letter was read asked all who cared to Volunteer for this larger service tb seep across the line. There was a moment of dead silence as the men considered their increased duties and risks.. Then a cheerrvoice broke the silence. James Ashton waving his hat stepped over the line and cried, "Come along, lads, death or glory." To a man the privates followed him over the cbalk. The incident bound them together with a stronger tie and closed in good cheer and enthusiasm. James Ashton lived for half a century after that but to the end of the chabter he was "Old Death or Glory,"and the wit of one of his comrades provided him with an epitaph long before he needed one "Here lies Death and Glory by his side." DANGEROUS ZEAL

Now and again the fervour of the Bluebacks ran to the point of becoming almost dangerous. An excess of loyalty and a sword ready for use may easily lead a man to anger. " Captain " said Paul Greenwood running breathless to his chief one day, "I heard a man cursing the King just now ; should I have him run through?" "No Sir"said the witty Captain " You Should only have s(e)cured him." It was a risky-pun, for the difference between ' skewering and running him thro' is not apparent. The gallant captain was prepared to take risks however and failed to see that a misunderstanding would have been of much consequence. The first public appearance of the Volunteers took place on the 30th of April, 1798. • They had drilled for weeks in private and now appeared before the gaze of their admiring townsfolk in the full glory of their uniform. The scene of this parade was the field on which St. Paul's church now stands.

WARRINGTON TRAINING COLLEGE.

ANNUAL PRIZE DISTRIBUTION. The Shortage of Teaching Appointments.

There was a large gathering at the annual prize distribution in connection with the Warrington Training College, which took place on Saturday after- noon. Mr. J. G. Legge, Director of Education for Liverpool, presided and distributed the prizes to the successful students. There were also on the platform: the Principal (the Rev. Canon Stevens° a) and the following members of the College Council :—The Venerable Archdeacon Spooner (hen. secretary), the Revs. Canon Willis, J. A. Pattinson, IL B. Blogg, Lieutenant-Colonel Buckton, Mr. W. Scott-Barrett, Mr. H. G. Crews, Miss Ferriman and the students of both years. Those who accepted invitations to be present were: Councillor Fulton-Smith (chairman of the War- rington Education Committee), Mr. .1. Moore Murray (director of education in Warrington), Mr. G. H. Mockler (director of educaticn in Wigan), the Rev. and Hon. C. F. Cross, the Rev. A. J. Brett, the Rev. J. Collins, the Rev. J. T: and Mrs. Darwall, the Rev. J. and Miss Davies, the Rev. H. B. Firth, the Rev. and Mrs. Garnett, the Rev. and Mrs. Lee-Jones, the Rev. G. and Mrs. Vale-Owen the Rev. B. and Mrs. Perci,al, the Rev. T. A. J. Ridpath, Dr. and Mrs. Bowden, Dr. and Mrs. Langdale, Dr. 4rs. and Miss Adams, Dr. Somervell. H.M.1., Mrs. Ruckton, Mrs. Crews, Mrs. Stevenson, Mrs. Willis, the Hon. Mrs. Willis, Mrs. Fulton-Smith, Mr. Amos, Mrs. and Miss Broadbent, Mr., Mrs., and Miss Burge,s, Mr. and Mrs. Brownell, Miss Batker, Miss Brookes, Mr. C. B. Baker, Mr. G. La Touche Baker, Mr. and Mrs. G. Brown, Miss Brown, Mr. and Mrs. Carruthers, Miss Coxou, Mr. Campagnac, Mrs. and Miss Davies Mr. Darby, Mr. K. Fortes, Mr. and Mrs. C. Time- well, Mr. C. and the Misses White,Mr. and Mrs. Wilson, Mrs. and Miss Woods, Miss Watkins, the Miss, s Woods, the resident members of the staff, the Rev. 11. A. Lester (lecturer) and about eighty parents and friends of the second year students.—Numerous apologies for non- attendance were received.

CANON STEVENSON AND THE SHORTAGE OF

APPOINTMENTS. The PRINCIPAL, in reviewing the work of the college during the past year, said the work had gone on steadily and satisfactory, and they had obtained very good reports from the inspectors who bad visited the College and especially from H.M. Inspector, Mr. Dale, who had charge of the training colleges in that district. What really troubled those who had to do with training colleges at the present moment was not the work inside the college itself but the future of the students. They were face to face with a very serious shortage of appointments for students in training colleges. It did not apply to that college alone, but to colleges all over the land. Indeed lin thought he might say the pressure , was perhaps less felt in the very populous districts, whet.? the demand for teachers was naturally greater than in some cither part sof the country. Still, the fact remained that last year and this year for the first time for many years they had had considerable difficulty in 1 finding appointments for all their students. His mmory went back to a period twenty-three or twenty-four 1 years ago, when they bad a similar difficulty. He I remembered that in those days the student who got an I appointment as soon as she left college at £4O per year considered herself a very fortunate girl. (Laughter.) I He would not like to go to any one of his students now and propose that she should take an appointment for that amount, because although he was not generally afraid of what -they would say to him—(laughter)—he should have serious misgivings if he were to use such language as that. (Laughter.) The salaries had practi- cally doubled themselves since then, but they were now face to face with a similar crisis. 111 r. Runciman (Minister of Education) thought that it. would very quickly pass away, and they all hoped that he was right. At the same time the fact that the matter might right itself in a few years was no great consolation to those who were anxious that it should right itself at this particular moment. He trusted it would not be long before this difficulty passed.away. Canon Stevenson paid a tribute to the memory of two members of the College Committee who 'bad passed away during the year—Canon Holme, Vicar of Great Budworth, whose cheery presence and sympathetic help they would greatly miss' and Canon Cleworth, who defended the position of Church schools in Lancashire with such zeal. In conclusion be said that whatever measure of success and prosperity they had, by the blessing of God, experienced during the past year, it was most largely due to the loyal and ever-ready help of those who worked with him in that College. He ' would like to make public acknowledgment of what was to him a matter of daily thankfulness and happiness

(Applause.) Mr. J. G. LEGGE, after expressing his own high regard for the College and its Principal, said that Liverpool owed to that College many of it best teachers, and it was one of the incentives to the young people of Liverpool in working up for the teaching profession that they might one day find admission to the Warrington Training Colleges. He was sure that Liverpool would be one of the last places in the country uhich would refuse to recognise the immense debt due to all Church of England and other denomi- national colleges in the country. He confessed that when he heard so much said about it being the duty of young people who aspired to be teachers to' prepare themselves for the service of others, he could not help thinking that at times they ought to reserve a little thought for themselves. The life of thetteacher was in many wars a missionary life. Teachers were always giving, but what he wanted to impress upon them was that what they gave could not,be of very much value unless some of it at lost was their own. Some time ago he read a hook called "'rho Training of the Imagination." The uriter—a poet and an old school- master—declared that the ideal of education was to train., the imagination and he said that what they should aim at in their own studies and in their lessons to others was to raise themselves or their pupils to such a pitch in any subject they were mdling, that they got positive enjoyment out ,of it. It was the moment where routine ended and enjoyment began that they should aim at. He had been reading the delightful lecture recently given at, that College by Dr. Somervell, in which he said they all "bad a silly spot somewhere." (Laughter.) That was profoundly true—with n any of them it was a large spot— (laughter)—hut still there was no-one who had not also got some earnest spot, and what they ought V) do was to find out what was their earnest spot and cultivate it. He hoped he would not be shocking the Principal,, or any future head master or bead mistress who might be present, when he begged of the students every day for a short time to try and forget everything their lecturers or teachers had said to them in the course of the day. (Laughter.) It was a wholesome exercise for them to retire into a sort of little temple of their own, and there give rein to their own imagination and pleasures. At their age, one of the greatest English poets, Emily Bronte, had practically finished writing all the poetry she wrote, and at their age they ought to be writing poetry, not necessarily for publication. (Laughter.) 'those of them who could not spend that useful little period when they were forgetting all about lectures and school in writing poetry, could spend it in other intellectual pursuits. In a little book he read some time ago which emanated from a training college, it was pointed out that there were two categories of teachers, the "quick and the dead." It was only by nurturing the vital spark within themselves that they could become quick and not dead teachers. In conclusion, Mr. Legge again urged the students in remembering their duty to others not to forget their duty to themselves, and in conceding so much as they' were asked to do to others, to reserve a little of their life's interest for their own self. cultivation. (Applause.)"

Mr. SCOTT BARRF:TT, in proposing a vote of thanks to Mr. Legge, said that the County Council was spend- ing large sums of money on schools end they were almost daily increasing in numbers. Although for some little time there might not to employment for all the teachers who sought it, be was certtin that so long as the Warrington Training College meintained its present high level, if any teachets were qualified and would get places, it was those who came from that College. (Applaitse.) Ihe Rev. H. 13. BLOC G seconded, and the vole was heartily parsed. Mr. LEGGE, in responding, said that with regard to the shortage of appointments, he agreed with Mr. Runciman that it was a temporary difficulty and he was equally sure that colleges such rs Wallington and several others he could name in the neighbourhood, would not seriously suffer. The best that it would do was to give a rather long holiday to some young ladies, and he did not know that they would not be better for having a little time in which to retire to that temple he bad spoken of. (Laughter.) One read in the papers a great many letters about the quality of the teaching in the schools. He made bold to say that a great deal of what they read in the papers on that topic was sheer nonsense, and he declared that the teaching given in our schools to-day was better than it ever was and that year by year the teaohers coming out of the training colleges were better than they ever were. His authority for that was not his own opinion but that of many of the most experienced heal teachers in Liver- pool, whose lives in their youth were not cast in tye pleasant places that young people now found in training colleges. These head teachers were the survival of a very severe regime—the fittest only survived in those days—and they assured him, with all their natural human prejudice in favour of the hard tip-bringing that they had, that the quality of the teaching was better to-day than ever it was. (Applause.) After the meeting the visitors were at liberty to inspect the College. Messrs. Crosfleld's band played selections on the lawn and tea was served in the library and in the schoolroom. Service in the College chapel brought the day's proceedings to a close.

OUR MISCELLANY.

The State of Washington, U.S.A., has made it a misdemeanour to give or receive a tip in either hotel, railway, or place of business. An American newspaper poet breaks into verse at the prospect. Let my travelling all be done In the State of Washington.

Swell cafes I need not shun, I should fear no waiter's dun,

From hotels I need not run In the State of Washington. Dimes and quarters need I none, Liberty at last is won. '

The millennium's begun In the state of Washington ** * *

In the article which appeared recently describing Boothbank and its Methodist associations, we stated that the tea pot— a relic of the days of Wesley and his travelling preachers, had disappeared. In this, it seems that we were in error, for the pot is the prized possession of Mr. W. Taylor Moss of Lymm, who has received,it by direct inheritance from the original possessor. It is understood that the tea-pot was the gift of Wesley himself to the hospitable tenant at Boothbank and bears his portrait in profile and the text Only let your conversa- tion be as it becometh the gospel of Christ." ** * *

It is pleasant to renew the acquaintance of the friends of our youth and the "Montreal Standard" has done us a service in bringing back to us the original Mary. We refer of course to the Mary who had the little lamb of spotless fleece. A genuine history lies behind the immortal "Mary had a little lamb." Mary was a village girl of Sterling, May. by surname S awyer, and her father kept sheep on his small farm. On a visit to the fold one day Mary was told that there was a dead lamb. "But it isn't dead" she protested, for -when' I move its head it will mov.e it back again." To prove her conten-

tion that the lamb was alive she asked to be allowed to take it home and nurse it back to health. The request was granted and the nursing was successful. By her kindness the little maid gained a new friend and com- panion. The lamb clung to her-and refused to be separated and it was out of this that there grew the episode of the lamb at school. John Rollestone, a friend, celebrated the event in a poem which ran:

IR * * *

Mary had a little lamb, His fleece was white as snow, And everywhere that Mary went The lamb was sure to go. He followed her to school one day, Which was against the rule ; It made the children laugh and play To see ,a.lamb at school. And so the teacher turned him out, And, still he lingered near; And waited patiently about Till Mary did appear.

This was the entire poem, bilt Mrs. Sarah Hale some years afterwards embellished it and added a moral of her own.

*"" * * *

Mary's lamb—for so it was known to the end of its days, never having had a name of its own—takes high ranks in the annals of quadrupeds. It not only provided by its conduct a nursery classic for all time, but continued after death to wield a decided influence for good. Mary Sawyer had a pair of socks made from the wool of her lamb, and as the poem had spread its fame the socks became valuable. There was talk of pulliag down the Old South Church in Boston and a committee was formed to raise a fund to prevent its destruction. To this Com- mittee Mary generously gave the socks. They were, however, regarded as much too valuable to be bought by any one purchaser, and were accordingly unravelled and the wool sold in tiny fragments for a total of £45. A further service which, the estimable pair did to humanity was to prove that kind- ness is conducive to longevity. The lamb succeeded in living twelve years and the maiden eighty-three—a sufficient refutation surely of the cynical proverb that the good die young. -

HOW GIPSY SMITH KEEPS MODEST.

Gipsy Smith, the famous Evangelist,has just returned to this country after his mission tour in America which Ins resulted in a great religious revival. He travelled 20,000 milts and addressed 600 meetings ; the audiences at many of them numbering 15,000 people. It would not be surprising if, after the tremendous success of his campaign, the Gipsy were "puffed up." But no, he is the same simple-hearted, kindly Gipsy we all know. He is exceedingly careful to avoid this danger, and during the mission tho audiences were told that the power to reach the souls of men and bring them to Christ comes from God alone. On his study table, in Cambridge, according to the" Free Church Chronicle," lies an old knife, a very common, ordinary looking affair, but invaluable to Gipsy, because it is the knife which long ago Gipsy's father used, when he folldltved his humble trade as a roving gipsy. "And now," says the evangelist," when I return from a great campaign, I pick up the old knife, and say to myself, If it were not for the grace of God I would still be using this as a wandering kipsy," The old knife, he claims, keep; down his pride,

BLACK JOAN.

BY S. BARING-GOULD.

Author of "The Vennycomequicks," "Atehalah," "Red Spider," "The Claverocks," "Richard Cable," &c.

At some little distance off the bold and precipitous coast at Lone, in Cornwall, lies the island of St. Nicolas. or Lone. It is nearly circular, and for the most part rises abruptly from the waves. A very dangerous and troublesome reef runs to it from the mainland, covered by the waves which lash themselves into foam over it. The sea-cliffs of the mainland rise high above the water, but there are in them n rents, down which streams brawl into little coves. The river that enters the sea at Lone divides the town into two .parts, each of which in the old days of rotten boroughs returned two members to Parliament.

The whole of this coast offered great advantages to smugglers, and at the beginning of last century the two Inoes were notorious nests of those daring seamen who carried on a contraband trade with France, and the difficulties presented by the coast made it most arduous and discouraging work for the preventive officers to check this "free trading.". So unsuccessful had they been, that at length it dawned on the minds of those in authority :,hat it was as well to plant a station on Looe as they felt convinced that the two denizens of the islet were in constant communication with the smugglers, and gave them notice whenever there was danger of their running their goods. They also shrewdly suspected that on Looe Island these goods were stored to be shipped ashore at suitable moments. Yet, however minute the search made on the island, no place could be found where the kegs of brandy and bales of silk were -secreted. The gravest suspicions attached to the denizens of the isle, Old Finn and his darfghter, Black Joan, but nothing could be brought home to them to justify their arrest. 'The coastguard station erected on Looe Island was planted there as much for the sake of watching the movements of ths Finns, as of observing the coast.

The story of Finn •;-vas peculiar. For some crime or other he had been outlawed, and had taken refuge on a rock in Plymouth Sound, where he spent some twenty-five years, not in absolute solitude, for in some unaccountable manner lie induced a woman to come out to his rock and become his wife. As a result of this wild union, a single child arrived, whom the couple called Joan. At length the woman died, and then Finn abandoned his rock and came ashore, where he was not molested. But life on the mainland was intoler- able to him, constant association with his fellow- men unendurable, and he fled to Looe Island with his daughter, there to end his days. lie was now an old Man, and Joan was a buxom, brown faced, sturdy young woman of three-and- twenty, with very black hair and eyebrows and dark eyes. It was not often that she appeared in the streets of Looe, and then only to • furnish herself with necessaries. She seemed to be flush of money. But—asked the preventive men—whence cams it? She and her father cculd earn it in one way only, by mating themselves serviceable to the daring men who defied the law.

When the station wa9 erected on the island, and two coastguards were placed in it, Finn and his daughter looked each ether in the face, and each face bore the aspect of dismay. "Never mind,Joan," said the old man; "we'll be even wi"em. Providence hey' given us brains by the ounce, and to them by the scruple. Don't go for to look sour at 'em. Put on your sweetest, and we'll do 'em yet." "I'll do my best, vayther, but it's a sore trial." "Man is born to trouble," replied the old man, sententiously, "as the waves is to fret, and the clouds to drop water. Whatever you do, don't let 'em get a mite of an inklin' as to where the cellars be." "Trust me, vayther."

Now, it happened that these cellars were full, and it was necessary to have them cleared, but how to do so with those coastgoardsmen on the alert was the puzzle. _

Igioe Island is a mass of old red sandstone, with gravel on top, due to the decomposition of the rock. Wliat had been done was to dig down under Joan's bed, and tunnel in the friable shale and gravel, and even in the rock, and to form chambers in which a good deal of contraband stuff could be stowed away. But, owing to the activity and sharp- ness of the officers, for some time the smugglers had been unable to clear out the cellars, that they might be free to receive a fresh supply. "Of them. two as is sot here a-spyin' on uo," pursued old Finn, "to my thinkin' aoel French is the wust. lie's gloomy and cross-grained, and I can't make no headway with ho; Joe Symmonds is a better sort of a chap—but he's sharp too." "I've cached he a-rollin' of his eye my-ward's," observed Joan. "He's a tidy chap, and would be friendly, but for his dooty." "Work that there eye, lovie," said the old man. "Summut may be done wi' it, if you keep a-workin' of it. in on --1“ "Joel

One evening Joan rushed in pn the father. " Vayther !" said she, breathlessly. French hey gone ashore." "No—never I Then, • now's our time; but how be we to manage 'vi' the tother ? " "Leave him to me," said Joan. "They're on the look-out ashore, I suppose?" "They be. They've signalled."

_ "Then signal back toe ready smart, and you, Joan, manage Joe Symmonds." .A quarter of an hour later, Joan ran to the coastguard's station in a state of the utmost excitement, her black hair flying in the wine. "Oh, Mister Symmonds—Mister Symmonds l* she gasped. "Whatever will rayther and me do? There is our boat broke loose, and the tide it running out and she'll be lust." She had, more- over, been lured by Symmonds away from the watch, and thus had allowed the officers of the Crown to. steal upon the smugglers unobserved. WOMEN'S CHAT.

Nowt for Women.—The scarcity oT public memorials to women has often been remarked, and it recalls a story told by a well-known Country parson. He was sent for by a dying parishoner, who had always bardened her heart against him, and his church. He found her most contrite, and did his best to- comfort her remaining hours, ofTerine up an earnest prayer on her behalf. When it came to the ending—Amen—she roused up. Aye," she said, "That it! Its a' tor men, and not for us poor women in this world." That, atany rate,is the case with our public statues and monuments. There are about one hundred of these memorials in London, and, apart.front Royal personages, only one is erected to a worean—that at Patldington Green in memory of Mrs. Siddons, the actress, which was unveiled by the late Sir Henry Irving. Among the memorials to Royal personages, there are in the ructropolis, three to Queen Victoria, two to Queen Anne, and one to Queen Eli zabeth.

Appropriating Distinguiahed Names.—There are ways of honouring heroes and distinguisbed personages other than by erecting monuments to their memory. Welling- ton's name was appropriated for a boot, Mr. Gladstone bestowed his on a bag, Queen Anne gives hers to the state of being defunct. It would be curious to linow how the saying "dead as Queen Anne" came into vogue, but it, is as common as the term "dead as a door nail." Miss Florence Nightingale is one of the very few women who have bestowed their names on articles of attire, and that too is not. very well known. At a recent bazaar in London, there were some "nightingales" on sale, not song birds, but a simple st3le of bed-jacket invented by Miss Nightingale for the use of her patients in the Crimea.

Royal Guests for Goodwood.—Prepar..tions are being made for the customary royal visit to Goodwood [louse for the race week the end of this month, which p-o- -ceeds the regatta week at Cowes and ends the Londe season: This year the King and ]] Queen, with Prine?ss Victoria, will be the guests of the Duke of Richmond and Lady Helen Gordon-Lennox for the lacing Week, whilst the Prince and Prince s of Wales are to stay at West Dean Park, with Mr. and Mrs. Willie James. The apartments se t aside for royalty at Goodwood [louse occupy a wide wing on the groend floor, end open cut on the terraced gardens. The house itself cannot lay claim to anything in the way of architectural beauty, but tic interior itself is full of I rieders treasures, tapestries and old-world furniture. The Cowes week will be notable for the visit to the Solent of the Czar and Czaritza of Russia,with U eir children, end possibly also the King end Queen of Spain. The Ithssian•royal yacht is expected to arrive on August 2nd, the bank holiday, and the regatta week promises to be exception- ally biilliant from a social point of view. Table Manners.—Among many other things of which we hoist imthis cultured age are ourimproved table manners, but there is nevertheless plenty of- scope for the lady who has started a school wherein the etiquette and art of eating are taught. There are a good many people who appear to be perfectly well-17red, and well mannered, but a hose habits at table are not above criticism, and it is at such times that we are most sensible of the shortcomings of our fellow beings. In medimval times people were not fastidious, and any such faults did not n atter. The Stuarts did much to refine British table manners, in respect of which Mary Queen of Scots compared favourably with her rival Queen Elizabeth, and James 1. continued the more cultured traits of his Royal mother. Yet we lead that in the following reign of Charles I. it was necessary for the Lord Chamherlain to issue regulations for the benefit of those invited to dine at the Royal table, and among other things they were not to come half-drunk, not to drink more than one goblet to every two dishes, not to lick their fingers, or throw the bones under the table.

An Arrusing Competition.—At the Actors' Fete held last week at the Botanic Gardens, a bat-trimming con- test by men took place, withessel by the Princess Royal and the Princesses Alexandra and Maud. It was an interesting performance. To begin with, there are few things more amusing than watching a man threld a needle—provided of course he is well behaved and omit: to say exactly what he thints *hen the thread dodges persistently each side of the eye. Then the average man never ccrisijers how a hat is likely to look upon a head. There is the hat, there is the trimming, he has to thread a reedlc—the hardest part of all—and then to sew those trimmings on, and generally he sews them as securely as he would a button on his coat. For pins, and the light tacking approved by skilled hands, he has a great contempt. alibi refuses to consider fo'r a moment the mel its of the, bandeau. Ribbon bows he can generally manage, flowers and foliage be finds more bothersome, while the arrangement and thing of a feather baffles him beyond deicript ion. The Summer Sales.—The silos are now in full swing;, and those among us uho have waited for them to pro- vide for the summer, are revelling in the thought that not only are we getting wbat we require at reduced prices, but that up to the present nothing airy-fairy would have been of much use. Headgear of all sorts and conditions is going exceptionally cheap, a fact due no doubt to the forthcoming changes. Again the very delightful ready-made cotton and linen blouse suits are priced ridiculously low, While many bargains are to be had in underskirts nod underwear generally. Indeed taken altogether the Summer Sales should prove a very happy hunting ground to the girl or woman possessed of good judgment and a cool head. Fine Troning.—The girl who can "get up" her "fine things " at home will rejoice at the return of the white petticoat, plentifully adorned with 'broderie Anglaise. Of course if such garments visit the laundresq, one might just as well, from an economic point of view, *ear light silk undershilts, but " dressed "at howie, the washing petticoat comes very much cheaper. Fine ironing is an art that should be mastered by every girl possessel of only a small dress allowance, for not only are the actual costs of the cleaner or laundress saved, but the garments themselves are likely, if carefully "got tip" at home, to afford a very much longer spell of usefulness.

Modish Neckwear.—ltuffles abcund,and very beautiful are the best and daintiest. Almost every airy-fairy gown has a ruffle slnt home with it, matching and blending of comae, and looking as though just blown together. Needless to ray, there 'is little wear to Fe expected of these delightful t rifles, they fade and droop very like hothouse flowers Ent while frrsh they are just the most fascinating items imaginary. Net, with rather large chenille spots in some contrasting colour. makes up exceedingly well into these modish neck ruffles, bat the home worker must see to it that she does not stint the quantity—which is considerable—of net required.or the effect will be poor and unsatisfactory.- White or Cream.—When hovering betwixt white and cream, choose the latter. This advice—coining from one of our greatest modistes—is invaluable, as the _majority who carefully go into the mater speedily dis- cover. Not one girl or woman in a hundred his a com- plexion that looks well acainst a mass of dead white, which has an unpleasapt habit of drawing out and pub- lishing all the sallow tones in the skin. A warm cream, on the contrary, is becoming to almost every girl and woman, end especially so to the women who has long since left youth behind. Trimmed with lace of " old" hue, an pearl embroideries, a soft warm cream gown is perhaps the most becoming and altogether charming toilette for the middle-2ged, or elderly, ever devised. MADGE.

REV. H. DUNNICO ON PERILS OF

THE AGE.

Preaching to a large congregation in Kensington Chapel, on Sunday night, the Rev. Herbert Minnie() said that .one of the greatest perils of this age was that a looserfein na3 given to every kind of appetite, and an increased spirit of wasteful luxury was to be seen on every band. The list of those slain every year by intemperance was more dreadful, es well as more numer- ous, than the victims of inhuman monsters like the Czar of Russia and the King of the. Belgians. This one evil alone was also responsible for a wasteful, extrava- gant expenditure amounting to well nigh a million sterling a day. Let them think for a moment of the enormous salaries commanded by music hall aitistes, without pronouncing any opinion as to the character of the items presented. The "artistes" were, for the most part, void of genius, often of average mental ability, and were as far remov-d from artistes like Forbes Yobertson, Sir Herbert Tree, and Miss Ellen Terry. as street pavement "artistes"were re:uoved from a Raphael or a Turner. Moreover,it was hardly possible to pass a bookstall without finding -exposed for sale a type of literature that deliberately ministered to depraved tastes. And yet, in the midst of it all, they had the pad spectacle of reit and women bearing the name of Him vkho wept over a city, not more wicked than our own, exhibiting all the characteristics of South Sea Island Savages. It was a sight to make devils laugh ant angels weep. "Might God forgive them."

BRITISH AGRICULTURE AND FREE TRADE.

Probably nine grown persons out of every ten in the United Kingdom arc well aware that the central and all-dominating Fiscal and political issue in the country remains what it was in the 1906 General Election, and must so remain until, at least, the ..next General Election. That issue lies mainly between the agricultural interests on the one band and the manufacturing interests on the other. The chief question is: Cdn,the mass of British wcrking people be induced, by the promised advantages of the Tariff Reform policy as a whole, .to submit to taxation of food on the scale proposed by the Tariff Reformers ? Let us first examine very briefly what would be the burden of.the food taxes on the averag . working man, and next Consider what the Tariff - Reformers argue and promise by way of compensation for the burden.

===== 0002601_19090703_art0089.txt | included=true | included =====
FOOD TAXATION CONSIDERED AS AN

INCOME TAX. • Compare the incidence of the proposed food taxation on the average working-class- family with the present incidence of the Imperial Income Tax on a family of the lower middle class.

- The food taxation now generally advocated by Tariff Reformers .is 5 per cent all round. Now it has been found on painstaking investigation by competent authorities that the average family with an income of 30s. a week down to 20s. or under has to expend two- thirds of its income on food. A simple arithmetical calculation shows that a tax of 5 per cent, on the food is equal to a tax of 3 1-3 percent. on.the total income. Take, on the other hand, a family of the lower middle class with au income of £2OO per annum. The Imperial Income Tax on such an income was (until last year, when a difference was madc. between earned .and unearned incomes) Is. in the £, or 5 per cent. But of course, the tax is only charged upon one-fifth of the total £2OO income—the other four-fifths being exempted. , Thus the charge on the total income is only-at the-rate of one-fifth of Is. in the X. being 1 per cent.

We see, then, that the food taxation on the total income of the working-class family would be 3 1-3 per cent., as compared with the Income Tax of only 1 per cent. on the total income of the lower middle-class family. That is; the burden on the smaller income would be 3 1-3 glees the burden on the larger income. It is true that therfood taxation would fall also on the middle-class income. But A family with an income of .i.:200 per annum does not need to spend nearly so large a proportion of its income upon food as.the family with .an income of 20s. to 3f_ts. per Ireek. gothing, WouSe.rent, and other items are, in the great majority of cases, a much larger proportion of the total expenditure of the lower middle-class than of the .working class ; aml food taxation does nUt touch on these other items.

Plainly, therefore, the food taxes must fall much more heavily on the working class than does Is: in the Income Tax 'on the middle class. Yet, as everyone knows, notonly the lower middle class, but the upper middle class also, constantly complain of time Is. in the .t income Tax, as cruelly oppressive. Moreover, it is obvious enough that the smaller the working-class income happens to -be--the further it falls below 30s. a week or 20s. a week--the more heavily the burden of the food taxation must press. There eau be no " exemptions " from the food taxes as there are from the Income Tax. The food will be alike proportionately dearer to the • woman factory worker with lOs. or Is. per week, and to the Old-Age Pensioner with is. per week.

WHAT DO TARIFF REFORMERS RESPOND ?

- To dim points the answers of Tariff Reformers are inconsistently various, according to the kind of audience which has; to be persuaded. When they are addressing agricultural audiences, they dwell on the advantage to be gained from the partial exclusion, of competing foreign agricultural produce. But, on the other hand : .1, The town-worker. is assured that •so small a tax as 5 per cent upon 'foodstuffs imported into this country , will not perceptibly raise average prices, because the foreign producers of the food will lower their average prices in order to Maintain their sales to us. Also, the " Colonial preference " given by us to agricultural produce imported from Canada, India, and Australia will so far stimulate producers in these countries that after a season or two they will be able to sell to us even more cheaply than they sell at present. 2. The town-workers are also promised that, for such small rise in food prices as may occur, they will be Compensated by the reduction of taxation in other directions.

3. More important still, the town-workers are told that, by reason of the Tariff exclusion of quantities of the foreign manufactures now imported into this country, they—the town-workers—will get higher wages and regular work, worth much more than ally probable rise in food prices.

===== 0002601_19090703_art0090.txt | included=true | included =====
THE FREE TRADE RETORT

If these remarkable predictions of Tariff Reformers be true, then it is clear that the agriculturists of the United Kingdom are being woefully befooled and defrauded. They certainly expect the 5 per "cent. duties on imported food to raise by the same amount the prices realised by home-grown produce. They have no other reason in the world for supporting the Tariff Reform policy. On the contrary, they have very strong reason for vehemently opposing it, because :

1. A Tariff Reform proposal is to impose tariff duties of 10 per cent. or 15 per cent. on agricultural machinery imported from abroad into this country. These imports increased from £360,000 worth in 1961 to A:770,000 in 1907. Al 5 per cent. duty on the latter amount would be a direct tax of 3:115,000 per annum on British agricultural industry. Obviously, also, the farmers would suffer more or less, along with the remainder of the community, by the Tariff-duties upon imported textile and other foreign manufactures. 2. British agriculturists themselves must pay a :considerable share of any taxes put upon foodstuffs imported from abroad. '1 his truth was demonstrated by experience of the is. per qr. duty on corn imported in 1902-3. When a. deputation waited upon Mr. Balfour in 1903" (May 15th) to urge that the duty Should be- retained, he pointed out that the total revenue raised by the tax in the year was about 500,000 ; and that, out of this, the portion which fell upon British agriculturists in regard to the imported feeding stuffs for -their live stock was tsS2,ooo—or nearly one-fourth of the total. Probably the -Tariff Reform proposals as they stand will tike at least :C1,000,000 per annum directly out of the pockets of British agriculturists. Can We suppose the latter are so unintelligent or so lacking in political influence that they will fail to exact -full compensation for their loss ? If they find that the commencing food taxes do not repay them, will they not insist strenuously upon a higher scale of duties than the 5 per cent. at present proposed?

===== 0002601_19090703_art0091.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ALTERNATIVES.

If the mass of working-class voters in the kingdom declare by a majority against food taxation, as they (lid in 1906, then, of course, the farmers will not ,get their 5 per cent, protection; and unless- they get it they have power enough—landowners and farmers together—to block the whole Tariff Reform policy. But if the majority of working-class voters could be persu4ded to face the 5 per cent. food tax, in the hope of getting the fuller employment and higher wages promised, then the, Tai if Reform. policy would be tried. In that event the working-class voters would have only themselves to thank when they fotuid, as other countries have found, that. tariffs begin small and grow. rapidly large; and that the cost of living, therefore, increases constantly faster than any possible ilium° in, their melee of wages.

Stretton

FARM LABOURER'S THEFTS. —At the Cheshire Quarter Sessions on Monday,'John O'Neil, 55, labourer, pleaded guilty to breaking into a labourer's shanty at Porch House Farm, Acton Grange, on June 9th. and stealing a pair of boots, thd property of Thomas Giblin ; also to breaking into a shanty at Birley Hayes Farm, Appleton, on June 11th, and sleating a vest, a silk handkerchief and a razor, the property of James Murray; and also to breaking into a shanty at. Moston Hall Farm Stretton. on June 12tb, and stealing an overcoat and a vest, the property of Edward Cosgrove.—Mr. R. Harrison, who prosecuted, said the prisoner pawned most of the articles in Warrington, stating that ho bad fiought them at Altrincham.—Prisoner was sent to gaol for nine months with bard labour. LONDON BRIDGE POTATO EXCHANGE.—The annual me eting of persons interested in the above took place on Monday evening in tbe Schoolroom, Stretton,,there being a fair attendance of farmers. The chair was taken by Mr. Richard Billington, of Stretton. who read letters of apology for non-attendance from Mr. J. C. Parr, Mr. George Egerton Warburton. and the Hon.' and Rev. C. F. Cross. The 'Chairman alluded to the excellent work the committee had done during the past year. The field during the last potato season, was not large enough to hold the cart loads of potatoes that were sent, and he thought the accommodation should be extended. The children were a great annoyance to them as they would creep under the carts to pick the •potatoes. He often wondered how they escaped being hurt. —Thanks were accorded to Mr. Piers E. Warburton, of Arley, and Mr. J. Yarwood for the use of the field; Mr. J. Whitlow for his kindness in opening the road for traffic; to the potato exchange, and to the committee for the services they had tendered during the past year. —The following were re-elected the committee : Messrs. ===== 0002601_19090703_art0094.txt | included=true | included =====
Padgate.

PADGATE WALKING D:71.-Y.—The very mention- of Walking Day makes our hearts throb with pleasure and brings back to our minds the thoughts of many similar days when we were young. This event is looked forward to as a red letter day by the children for months beforehand and it was gratifying to all on Saturday morning last to find that the weather which bad been so unsettled for days had to a certain extent cleared up. With a blue sky above and the sun shining brightly there was every appearance of a beautiful day. That this was very mach appreciated by the children was evidenced by their merry faces and joyous laughter when they assembled together in front of *the Wesleyan Chapel. The procession consisted of the two religious bodies in the district viz., the Wesleyan and Church of England Sunday schools, and it is customary for each body to head the procession every alternate year and this year the honor fell to the Padgate Wesleyan. Punctually at 2.15 pm. the procession headed by the Industrial School Band started from the Wesleyart Chapel and proceeded along Padgate-lane as far as the Padgate Boundary returning by the same route along Padgate-lane as far as Fearnhead Cross and then back to the church. Here the procession halted for a few minutes whilst tbe band played the National Anthem each section going to their respective schools for tea the members of the band being entertained by the leaders in the procession. Several of the children appeared in costumes representing different characters which added unusual interest to the procession and caused much amusement to the onlookers. Beautifully decorated lorries bad been lent by the farmers of the district in which were seated the younger portion of the scholars, and many of the inbahittints bf the village had hung out flags and bunting. in honour of the occasion. After tea the scholars proceeded to their respective fields where sports, games, &c.,were indulged in until dusk. The day throughout was fine although dark clouds hung overhead during the earlier part of the afternoon but cleared awayolater. A very enjoy- able and interesting day was spent and thoroughly enjoyed by all who took part in it.

PRESENTATION TO COUNCILLOR W. B. ROBERTS.— Councillor W. B. Roberts entertained a large gathering of Messrs. Greensll, Whitley, and Co., at the Mulberry Tree Inn, Stockton Heath on Saturday evening. Mr. John Hobin, she oldest employs, presented Councillor Roberts, on behalf of the men, with a handsome silver rose bowl, supplied by Messrs. Enstance and Co„ of Sankey-street, and an illuminated address, prepared by Mr. S. Welsby, of Stockton Heath. The address reads as follows W. B. Roberts, Esq. We, the employs of Messrs. Greeaall, Whitley and Co., Ltd., who have been under your immediate • supervision for the period of 23 years, during which you have been engaged as head . brewer, and from which position you are about to retire, take this opportunity to congratulate you on your attainment to a position which enable you to pass the rest of your life in retirement. We also desire to express lour sincere and heartfelt appreciation of the many kind acts, as well as the unfailing courtesy, which have always characterized your treatment of every workman under your charge. We wish you and yours health, happiness and long life in your retirement, and trust. that this token of our esteem and the small rose bowl accompanying it may serve to keep a sweet place in your memory for all your old employees. June, 1909." —Councillor Roberts suitably acknowledged the gift, and expressed the hope that the men would be as loyal to his successor as they bad been to him. Lie was much obliged to them for their kind gifts, which he very much appreciated.—A capital musical programme was submitted, the pianoforte accompaniments being played by Mr. Amos.

PARISH COUNCIL.

Immediately after the Parochical Committee, the quarterly Parish Council meeting was held. Mr. F. Pearson presided and there were present Messrs. J. Whitley, J. McDermid, G. J. Brown, J. Hallows, F. Warburton and E. Bradshaw (clerk).

===== 0002601_19090703_art0098.txt | included=true | included =====
SWING BRIDGE.

Arising out of the minutes Mr. Hallows asked whether any reply bad been received to their letter to the Manchester Ship Canal in respect to the state of the swing bridge in wet weather. The Clerk replied that a letter had been received, hut it was practically a mere acknowledgment of their letter. It further stated that the matter would have their attention.•

Mr. Hallows : I move that an urgent letter be written for it is simply disgraceful for anyone to go over that bridge in wet wenther.People have to walk in the tramway lines instead of on the footpath. Mr. Whitley said that it would be improved if they would raise the boards an inch at one end.

The Clerk said tint if they would instruct him to peg away at the Canal Company until they -did something, • he would do so.

Air. Hallows asked if it would not strengthen their hands if they got the County Council to dea! with the matter. A resolution for the County Council would have a better effect than it would if one were sent from that Council.

It was agreed that the clerk write to the Ship Canal Company urgently requesting them to take to remedy the matter.

VOTERS' LIST.

In replying to Mr. McDermid, the clerk said he always had the voters' lists put in a prominent place at the church and at the chapels. The one at the church remained until be put the one up the next year ; but those at the chapels were removed, a few days after they were put up.
