Hatton.

ACCIDENT TO A CYCLIST.—On Thursday evenine, a young man named Frank Priestley, of 15, Walterloo- road, Runcorn, was cycling through Hatton and in negotiating a very sharp turn by the Primitive Metho- dist Chapel, t.he scene of many accidents, he collided violently with a greengrocer's cart belonging to Mr. T. Lightfoot of Lower Walton. He lay unconscious for a considerable time and was conveyed to a house in the vicinity. Dr. Bowers was called in and stated that he was Suffering froth fracture of the left shoulder and severe bruises on the head, besides a great shock to the nervous system. The bicycle was rendered piactically useless. After some discussion it was decided to take the young man to some relatives who lived at Preston Brook, for the night, Dr. Carruthers of Halton, giving an outline of his injuries. It is considered high time that sem-thing was done to alter, this corner of the road, as it is almost impossible to, see anyone coming in the opposite direction.

MERIT BADGE AWARDED TO A PENKETH POLICE- MAN.—The merit badge has been awarded to Police- Constable Houghton, of Penketh, by the Chief Constable of Lancashire. The other morning, between one and two o'clock, the officer discovered a man named Warburton, of Ship-yard, Warrington. in Hill-lane, Penketh, carrying a bag. On seeing the officer, who at once gave chase, the man dropped the bag and ran half- a-mile across country before he was captured. The fugitive, who was armed with a heavy piece of wood , threatened to "do for" the constable if he approached him. A struggle took place when the constable got at close quarters' but the officer succeeded in handcuffing the man. fie was tried by the magistrates and sentenced to three months' imprisonment.

' TANNERY TRIP.—The Penketh Tannery trip took place on Saturday; when 335 employees and friends turned out early to take in the freshness of the morning and to embark for Southport by the 7.10 min from Fiddlers' Ferry Station, arriving at Southport at 9.45 Here they at once began to enjoy the many pleasures Southport furnishes. At 12.30 they all turned up to Fletcher's Cafe, Lord-street, to enjoy a good lour course dinner. Dinner over, the party continued to enjoy themselves in various ways until 7 o'clock, when they left sunny Southport behind for the return home, which was reached at 9.10. All were very grateful to Mr. W. B. Moston, the Tannery IF ecrotary and cashier, for the way in which he had managed, made and carried out the arrtingements for the trip.

A TRAM SMASH IN THE FOG

Warrington Tradesman Gets Damages.

On Tuesday at the Leigh County Court before his Honour Deputy-Judge Maxwell, Dennis Dolan, of 2, Antrobus-street, Warrington, wholesale _potato sales- man, sued the South Lancashire Tramways Company for damages for personal injuries and for injuries to horse, cart and harness. Mr. Arthur Browne, Warrington, appeared for plaintiff and said that Dolan on the 22nd January last had been round Croft and purchased a cart load of potatoes and then went round Golhorne Smithy and Lane Ends and on to Ashton-in-Alakerfleld and there disposed of the whole of his potatoes to several customers. He started for home in his empty cart from Ashton-in-Makerfleld about half-past six and it was a foggy night. He proceeded on his way, being stood up in his cart driving along the Warrington road, until he got within 30 yards of the Ashton-in-Makerfleld rail way station bridge, and was driving on his proper side when suddenly a great light flashed between his legs and on turning round hesaw a tramcar a yard or two away. Dolan shouted but in an instant the tramcar banged into his cart and pushed it three or four yards and then threw him bodily out on the footpath where be alighted with his breast on the curbstone. The horse was also thrown down. Mr. Dolan was dazed for some time and when be got up be' found that the horse had been injured and the cart damaged both back and front. He asked the driver (who never got off tbe car) for his number and name and address, hut the driver kept saying "Get out of the way." With the assistance of a man who was present but whose name and address the plaintiff had forgotten and had not yet discovered although he had advertized for him in two papers, he was then removed down the passage to the .racecourse. The harness was tied up and repaired and subsequently two pel ice-constables came on the scene to wi•om the plaintiff complained of being hurt but they did not think him sufficiently injured to see a doctor and advised him to proceed home. He walked with his horse and cart the seven miles home and proceeded straight to bed and did not get up until Monday morning the 25th January, when he went to his doctor who having examined him found that his breast bone was fractured and his knee badly injured and he ordered him at once into the infirmary where he was between life and death for a fortnight and remained there for a month. He had not yet recovered and was still in the doctor's hands. He had therefore lost his business and claimed substantial damages at his Honour's hands. The plaintiff bore out this statement and in cross- examination by Mr. Greaves Lord (instructed by Mr. Percy Marsh, Solicitor, of Liverpool) denied that his horse backed into the tram, and also denied that he had had anything more than one glass of beer at noon at Croft, and another at Ashton-in-Makerfleld when he had some sandwiches that he had taken Pith him. Five boys were called who corroborated the Plaintiff in a very clear manner. Counsel for the defence called the driver of the car who swore that he was only going two miles an hour and that he saw the cart in front of him about eight yards ahead. He followed it a distance of five or six yards and could see the plaintiff asleep lying at the bottom of the cart covered ' with sacks and with his bead looking towards him. Plaintiff suddenly woke up and pulled the horse and cart to one.side and backed into his tram. Witness only slightly bumped the cart and said a slight dinge was made on the car. Cross-examined by Mr. Browne he admitted that he was ten minutes late but he persisted that the plaintiff was never thrown out of the cart although the five boys bad sworn that he had been thrown out of the' cart and was on his hands and knees on the footpath and further that the horse's knees were badly bleeding. L'he Tram Conductor and other witnesses were called who contradicted one another, and his Honour without calling upon Mr. Browne gave a verdict for plaintiff £4O damages and costs and gave a certificate to Mr. Stanley Wright (of Messrs. Wright Garnett and Wright), for the plans.

" There are some piles (for mooring) in the River Thames which were driven in during the reign of Charles 1.," said counsel in a navigation case at the city of London Court on Wedesday, "and they are still good."

===== 0002601_19090703_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
LIBERAL MEETING AT WIDNES.

Mr. J.W. S. Cailia, secretary of the Financial Reform Association, was the principal speaker at an open•air meeting held on West Bank Vromenade on Tuesday night. Councillor Davis Lewis presiding. Mr. Caine said that Free Trade and Tariff Reform were the most important questions before the country. Free trade meant letting things come in freely, and allowing goods to COMP into this country simply because we wanted them. Great Britain was the only country in the world that had adopted Free Trade, and she had prospered considerably by so doing, for it had been the means of bringing this great Empire together. They had the biggest trade of any nation on the earth. More goods are sent out and more goods are brought in. Free Trade had been chosen because of the success that had been achieved.—Councillor Lewis said he was quite pleased with the Government's policy, because he firmly believed that they were trying to carry out reforms on the lines of social progress. They meant business, and it was to them alone to look for any reforms that would benefit the masses of people in this country. WIDNES SCHOLARS' SUCCESS.

NEWTON TORIES AND THE BUDGET.

Lord Wollner, the Conservative candidate for the -Newton Parliamentary.Divisicn, who is conducting a tour of the whole of the townships in the division, on Monday night addressed a meeting at St. Mark's school, Haydock. Mr. C. R. Pilkington presided.—Lord .Wolmer spoke on the question of the Army and Navy, and said that the country was certain to condemn the Government, which, after being three years in power, was raising taxation .by £13,000,000, and yet was not able to provide the country with an adequate Army and Navy. Lord Charles Beresford had told them that the Navy was not adequate for their needs, and Lord Roberts had told them that tiley were not prepared for war with the Army, and he fert that they could not only condemn the Budget for its n fairness, but also for the fact that it did not meet fillktional needs. On the other side, they, the Tory party,were prepared with a perfectly clear and definite policy, which would shift the burden from their shoulders. They would, tax the foreigner. The Budget marked the final and complete bankruptcy of Free Trade.

ST. HELENS.

ST. HELENS PRESBYTERIAN CHURCH.

Special re-opening services were held on Sunday at the St. Helens Presbyterian Church, which has recently been in the hands of the decorators. The interior of the church has been redecorated and renewed, and several improvements made, notably the introduction of electric lighting. The organ, which was built in 1887, has also been overhauled and a new tremulant stop added. The work of decoration has also extended to the adjoining schools, and has cost over £5OO, which was raised by means of a bazaar last autumn. The Rev. James Pringle, 8.A., minister, preached at the morning service, And in the evening the Rev. A. Connell, M.A., 8.D., of Sefton Park, Liverpool, preached to a crowded Congregation. MR. GLOVER, M.P., AT ST. HELENS.

Mr. T. Glover, M.P., addressed a large open-air meeting at Parr, St. Helens, on Monday night. Coun- cillor B. Fletcher presided. Mr. Glover spoke on the benefits of the Eight Hours Act, and, continuing, said that the Government of the day bad at last tackled the question-of land taxation, He claimed that it was entirely due to the advent of the Labour Party to Parliament that the Government bad done this. The Tories taunted the Governlnent tat they were at the beck and call of the Socialist party, as they were pleased to call the Labour party in the house. He did not care what they called the party if the Government only passed the leas s they advocated. The Government had brought in a Budget on lines which no other Government had ever attempted. Both the Liberal and the Labour parties were pledged to economy, but they had to find money for old-age pensions and also for Dreadnoughts. The Government was being driven to spend more on the Navy, but the method they had adopted to find money did not at all please the landowners. The old-ace pensions were benefiting no fewer than 6,500,000 old people, and it was the object of the Labour patsy to extend it, and to enable all the old people of seventy to come out of the workhouses and end their days in the homes of their friends. He trusted that the Govern- ment would adhere to their land taxation proposals, for that was the right linos on which taxation should go. The poor had too long borne the greater part of the burden, and in their efforts to help the workers and to ley a fairer burden on the shoulders of the rich the Government would have the whole-hearted assistance of the Labour Party.

INTERNATIONAL CORRESPONDENCE

SCHOOLS.

A meeting of the local students of the International Correspondence Schools Limited, Kingsway, London, took place at the IVinmarleigh Cafe, on Saturday, 70 students and friends, including several ladies being present. Mr. George Hickman, Divisional Superintendent,in a few remarks stated ,that the Warrington Division was opened in October 1508. and comprised the district of Wigan and St.Helens.He was pleased to say it now num= bered.24o students, 90 of whom belonged to Warring- ton. (Applause). He eas further pleased to say, since he had the pleasure of meeting them last, they had now enrolled 50 more, and it was very gratifying to him to meet them that eight to he able to say that in looking over their percentage certificates that Warrington Division stood out' as good, if not better than any Division in the North of England. He felt sure that providing they only continued as they were doing, Warrington would turn out men capable of holding some of the best positions employers had to offer, both in this country Red any other. (Applause). He further stated that he feltsure that not only himself,but others present that night, knew that the rule of thumb was dead and that the trained man was the only man now that had any chance of obtaining anything like a salary sufficient to enable him to lead a decent life and bold his position. Further he asked them to wear their I.C.S. buttons of which they might well be proud and to make the thing known to their friends, so many of %hoar were in need of the courses of instruction taught by the schools and to keep uppermost in their minds that splendid offer made by the Head Office, viz, the free trip to London which was one to be remembered by all who were successful in winning it. urning to the ladies present, he said he was pleased to welcome them to the gathering, for there was no doubt that women intended to take a very big hand in the affairs of the future, and the I.C.S. had some very good things to offer in scholarships for any young lady —which was to his mind very much better, and more remunerative than the work in mills and factories. He thanked them very much indeed on behalf of the schools, also his representatives and ',himself, and he hoped that they would all turn up at the annual outing which lakes place on the 10th July., in the:shape of a drive to Chester, lunch and tea.—After the Com- mittee's report, a smoking concert ended in a very pleasant evening. The following ladies and gedtlemen obliged by entertaining the company as follows : Selections on the piano, -Mr. Clay ; song, Mr. Bennett; selections by concertinas and clarionet, Mr. Wilde and friends ; song, Miss Cross ; song, Mr. Price ; piano- forte solo, 141 r. Clay; impersonations of seine well- known comedians, which were very 'well done by Mr. Bennett; song, Miss Cross.

A CANDID UNIONIST ON THE BUDGET.

Mr. Arthur Chamberlain's Striking Approval. --- , No Injury to Business.

Mr. Arthur Chamberlain, speaking at a meeting of the Birmingham Trust on Tuesday, submitted to the cold analysis of a business man the outcry of those who complained that the Budget will endanger the corn- meiclal interests of the country. "I look to the Budget," he declared," as one reason, and indeed my strongest reason, for hoping for an improvement in trade during the coming year. The Budget removes from labour some portion of its worst burdens, and in doing that it renders labour mole effec.. tive, and labour when more effective adds to the general wealth. I expect, therefore, this Budget, which I must call the gteetest Budget of modern times, to influence for geed the condition of trade. I say wit hou t fear of rat icnal contradiction that there Is no single line in that Budget which can do anything to depress anyone of our commercial and trading interests." Certainly, be proceeded, the way in which the Finance Bill dealt with land would not injure our commercial interests, for it would assist the process of putting on the land people who would, by intense cultivation, increase its productivenes beyond what was possible when it was held in Nast quantities in a single man's hand.

All the proposals of the Budget in connection with social Worm had a tendency end an intention to increase the efficiency of labour. They increased the productiveness of capital, and it was probable that after the Budget had Leen accepted by the nation some of those who now feared the loss of their wealth by reason of the new taxation would find the difference replaced by the power of wealth to obtain a larger interest in consequence of the increased productiveness of labour which the schemes of the Government had effected. "I hope, therefore," exclaimed Mr. Chamberlain, "none of you will he affected by the outcries of a few millionaires. Free Trade finance will not make anyone poorer, though it will make many richer. Free Trade will not rob anyone of that which fairly belongs to him, thoueh it will give ,to many some proportions of that which they ought to have had long ago. Lord Rothschild, he said, complained that the Budget provided too much money' and his Lordship thought that he and his friends should there- fore be relieved of the special taxes that affected them. ‘Vby ? If the Budget provided too much money why should not something come off the taxation of the poor? Why should it not come off the enormous tax that was now put upon tobacco? But he could not imagine the money that the Government might get in taxes being More than they would know how to spend, and be would much prefer spending it to taking it off taxes. For instance, he would like to see the age for pensions reduced. (Hear, bear.) Poverty, drink, and disease were poor things for manufacturers to work upon.

WALKING DAY. GLORIOUS WEATHER AND A SUCCESSFUL FESTIVAL.

The glorious sunshine of an ideal summer day brought joy to the hearts of the children of Warring- ton yesterday, for Walking Day—the festival of the year to the rising generation—had come round once more, and all doubts as to its success were dispelled by the perfect conditions. Throughout the day the sun shone resplendently from a clear blue sky, giving a crowning brightness to the streets which were looking gay with the decorations now to be seen on every hand in preparation for the Royal Visit. The little ones revelled in the pageantry and festivity -which held sway in the old town. The lapse of years only serves to enshrine Walking Day more deeply irr their regard, and he would be a bold man indeed who would venture to suggest its relegation to the oblivion of bye-gone events. His unhappy fate may be con- jecture from the storm of indignation whieh arose when the Town Council recently tried to tamper with the date of the festival.

From an early hour yesterday the town was astir. Children in all the glory of new finery were hurrying to the schools, and strains of music—of varying quality—filled. the air, for Walking Day is the bands- men's harvest. By nine o'clock many of the school processions were on their way to the respective rendez- vous_ The usual stereotyped arrangements were departed from this year. When the King and Queen are about to honour Warrington with a visit, the plot of greensward in front of the Town Hall—of which we are rightly proud—must look at its best, and therefore it was wisely decided not to allow the lawn to be used for the marshalling of the general procession. Instead, the schools entered the grounds by the East gate, passed in continuous order in front of the hall, and left by the Test gate. The children did not deplore the innovation, for the long wait on the lawn in a broiling sun has never been highly popular with them. There was one marked improvement. Instead of having two distinct processions along separate routes, the Roman Catholic schools fell in at the rear of the Church of England and Nonconformist schools and traversed the same thoroughfares. They did not pass in front of the Town Hall, as there would have been some difficulty in steering the large banners along the drives, but joined the procession at the top of Arpley- street. The spectators welcomed the change, for they we thus enabled to witness the complete procession from one point of advantage, and much unnecessary crowding and confusion was avoided. Owing to the desire to preserve a clear road through the town for traffic from the Cheshire side, the route was consider- ably altered, Wilson Patten-street being excluded from it. The advantage of having such a wide and hand- some thoroughfare as Bridge-street was once more strikingly demonstrated, for from Friars Gate to Bridge-street there was what might be described as a double procession; the chilgren marching up one side of the street and down the other. The result was an exceedingly pretty picture, and the onlookers were loud in their praises. The Chief Constable (Mr. M. Nicholls) is to be warmly complimented upon the smoothness with which everything passed off, for he had worked out the details with the greatest care and the fact that they were loyally adhered to,by the school. authorities ensured success.

Councillor Craik and Mr. R. T. Fairclough again acted as marshals.

On the Town Hall steps and the adjoining stands a large number of prominent townspeople had assembled. They included the Mayor and Mayoress (Alderman and Mrg. Forshaw), the Deputy-Mayor (Alderman Smethurst), Mr. and Mrs. Robert Pierpoint, and many members of the Council. Promptly to time the children of that ancient institution, the Blue Coat School, who always have the place of honour, made their appearance, and for close upon an hour the various Sunday schools passed on in quick succession, with very few stoppages. The order was as follows : (1) Blue Coat School and Band. (2) Padgate Industrial School and Band. (3) Fairfield School and Band of Bth. and 40th Regimental District. (4) National School. (5) St. George's Mission and Mr. Smich's band. (6) St. Clement's Mission. (7) St. James's School and Territorial Band. (8) Holy Trinity School and Walton Band. (9) Factory Yard Mission. (16) St. Luke's School.

11) St. Paul's School and Ardwick Green Industrial Band. (12) All Saints' Mission. (13) St. Barnabas' School and Preston Brook Band. (14) St. Peter's School and Mr. Littler's Band. (15) St. Ann's School and Derbyshire Imperial Yeomanry Band.

(16) liuckley-street and Crosfield's Band. (17) Emmanuel (R.E.) School.

, . (18) Ragged School and Band. (19) Salvation Army and Band. Always imposing as a demonstration of strength, the procession has not usually been notable for originality or picturesqueness, but this year some of the schools had made special efforts in this direction, and many pretty touches of colour called forth warm appreciation. A striking feature of St. Luke's display was a large floral crown, the streamers being held by little girls dressed in white, who also carried baskets of flowers. At the head of All Saints' School walked a fairy queen attended by a band of children who had silver wands surmounted with red, white and blue ribbons. With most of the schools, little maids carrying baskets of choice flowers figured prominently. St. Peter's school, which has a high reputation for attractive pageantry, excelled itself. A youthful cyclist was costumed to represent" Boy Blue," and there were troupes of flower girls with pretty floral sashes and garlands, merry gipsies, and groups of Swiss and Japanese in picturesque dresses. Several artistically decorated mail carts added additional variety. Buckley-street school also made a brave show, A contingent of Morris dancers tripped lightly along and next came a number Of beautiful floral crowns,an arch and an anchor. It was pleasing to note that this school made an effort on behalf of one of our most deserving charities, a collection being taken en route in aid of the Children's Cot at the Infirmary. There were several juvenile cyclists with handsomely decorated machines to which were attached receptacles for coins. As usual the central object in Emmanuel school procession was an open Bible carried under a handsome canopy on which were inscribed the words "The Secret of England's Greatness." The rear of this section of the procession was brought up by the Ragged School and the Salvation Army which do such splendid work amongst the very poorest cf the population. Many of the girls who walked with the Ragged School had been provided with new pinafores. Conspicuous in the Salvation Army con- tingent was a floral device with the words "Faith, Hope and Charity."

===== 0002601_19090703_art0116.txt | included=true | included =====
ROMAN CATHOLIC PAGEANT.

There was but one opinion of the Roman Catholic procession—that it excelled all previous efforts in general attractiveness. This is saying a great deal, for the members of this Church always excel in the beauty of their pageantry. That love of the ornate, which is such a striking feature of their religious system, finds full expression on this occasion, and the result is a spectacle of great brilliance. Yesterday, the spectators were frequently moved to express their admiration of what must have been the outcome of much painstaking preparation on the part of parents and teachers, by cordial applause. The schools marched in the following order : (1) St. Mary's, Buttermarket-street. (2) St. Alban's. (3) St. Benedict's. (4) Sacred Heart. (5) Our Lady's, Latchford. The children were splendidly disciplined, and whenever there was a momentary halt in the pro- cession, the little feet could be heard marking time with soldierly precision. There was a perfect forest of banners of exquisite texture and workmanship. Seven of them depicted the Sacraments, whilst others bore representations of Our Lady and the Saints. Here and there a glittering processional crucifix arrested the eye, and groups of altar boys, in cassocks and surplices, followed. As usual, the costumes and grouping of the children were most beautiful, each school working to some definite scheme. Many of the scholars wore sashes of various tts, while on their heads were garlands of choice flowei s. Wands tipped with blooms were generally carried, and a contingent ,of boys marched smartly along bearing miniature silver spears. There were several handsome statues of the Virgin and the Sacred Heart, embowered in flowers and greenery, but perhaps the greatest admiration was attracted by the statue of Our Lady which was borne aloft by- young ladies of St. Mary's Church. Above it was a magnificent floral arch and from the pedestal were suspended a large number of streamers which -were held by little girls who also carried baskets of flowers. Other features which helped to make up a gorgeous picture were floral harps, arches and other devices which were magnificently executed, and the whole scene was one calculated to live long in the auemories of the spectators.

===== 0002601_19090703_art0117.txt | included=true | included =====
HOW THE DAY WAS SPENT.

In the afternoon the children betook themselves to fields in the locality where they d4ported themselves right merrily. Most of the Nonconformist schools refrained from joining' in the procession, and made their way direct to the railway station, the object being to give the youngsters a full day in the country. Wycliffe scholars again visited Hartford; Bold-street children went to Lymm ; Silver street to Helsby ; Workingmen's Mission to Acton Grange; Cairo-street Unitarian to Southport; Golborne-street Baptists, Acton Bridge; Welsh Church, Southport; Ebenezer Church, High Legh. With such ideal conditions, the time passed all too quickly, and the Walking day of 1909 will long be remembered as one of the most successful in the history of the town.

Buggins, 0.5.8. As usual the procession presented a very picturesque spectacle. It was beaded by the Borough Band and next came the large and handsome banner of St. Alban. There were three other school banners—Sacred Heart, Our Lady and St. Joseph. The Guild of St. Agnes (girls) was preceded by the banner of St. Agues. Another beautiful feature was a statue of Our Lady. Before the infants was carried a handsomely decorated shield bearing the name of the schools. Choice bouquets and trailers of smilax were carried by the infants and by the girls of the senior school. All the infants were attired in white, with sashes of green and gold, and the girls wore green and gold rosettes and the boys gold caps. The girls of the senior school had wreaths of most roses on their heads and wore green silk sashes. They carried streamers of smilax intertwined with pink ribbon. The boys had red jerseys and white tennis hats. The afternoon was spent in a field at Padgate where games and sports were indulged in.

PENKETH WALKING DAY

Favoured with ideal weather, the Walking Day celebrations at Penketh yesterday afternoon were a complete success. Below we give the particulars of the arrangements for each school : _ _ _

PENKftli (WEitY,YAN) : Number of scholars on books, 137 boys, 103 girls; average attend trice, 80 morning, 170 afternoon; number of teachers, 37 ; minister Rev. F. W. Kernick, ; superintendents, Mr. L Withinshaw and Mr. J. Carman; secretary, Mr. Frank Davies. The procession was headed by Penketh Tannery Prize Band and five large banners (the first bearing the name of the schools) and several smaller ones were carried. Leaving the school at 2 P.m., the scholars marched along Warrington-road,up Heath-road, Greystone-road,through Mr. Dodds grounds,and through the village to Mr. Parker's, where they partook of lemonade, etc., and visited Mrs. Parker's Rose Garden. then they proceeded to school for tea at 4 p.m., und afterwards adjourned to the Recreation ground and indulged in sports and games. The sports were taken charge of by Mr. Parker's Bible class, and very suitable prizes were given to successful competitors at 9 p.m., the children returned to school, and gave hearty cheers for those who had helped to make the festival a success On leaving the school each child received a bag con- taining nuts, gingerbreads, sweets, etc. During the evening the bands played a large number of suitable selections.

ST. MARY'S, GREAT SANKEY :—Number of scholars on books, 631; number of teachers, 37; Vicar, Rev. J. R. Jones; who was accompanied by the wardens and sidesmen. The procession, headed ',s7Preston Brook Band, started at one o'clock from Sankey school and proceeded through Meeting House-lane and Penketh, thence to Sankey Bridges and back to Sankey. The teachers were - entertained to luncheon by the Vicar. Then the scholars had their tea in relays. Sports etc., were indulged in, in the Vicarage field after tea.

THE ROYAL YISIT.

FINAL ARRANGEMENTS.

Tuesday next will be a great day in the history of Warrington, for their Majesties the King and Queen are to honour the Borough with a brief visit. Every preparation is being made to extend a loyal and enthusiastic welcome to the Royal visitors, and the town, with its lavish decorations will wear a gala appearance. The arrangements are now complete and given real "King's" weather, the day will be one to be long and happily remembered by every inhabitant. The King will leave London by special train at 2.30 on Saturday afternoon for Rugby, and after fulfilling an engagement there, his Majesty will remain °for the week-end.

On Monday morning at 9.55 the Queen will leave Euston for Knowsley, calling at Rugby, where the King will join her Majesty. They will proceed together to Knowsley, and before returning to town on the evening of the 7th will fulfil long-Standing and important engagements at Manchester, Birmingham, and elsewhere. . OFFICIAL PROGRAMME.

We are indebted.to the Town Clerk (Mr. J. Lyon Whittle) for the following official programme : Their Majesties will leave Worsley Hall in motors at about 5.15 for Knowsley via Warrington. It will be necessary to proceed at a fast rate of speed, but it will be reduced at Some places along the ronte as follows in order that the public may see His Majesty : Arrive Irlam boundary 5.30., pass slowly through the town, stop for the Chairman of the Urban District Council to be presented. Leave Irlam 5.45.

Arrive Warrington (Bruche Bridge Manchester- road) 5.55 ;'pass slowly through the town to the front of the Town Hall, remain there long enough to allow of an address being presented (not read) to His Majesty and for the Mayor and Mayoress,Sir Gilbert and Lady Greenall and the member for the Borough (Mr. A. H. Crosfield) and Mrs. Crosfield to be presented to His Majesty. Miss Edith Forshaw will have the honour of pre- senting a bouquet of flowers to the Queen. Miss Bessie Forshaw will have the, honour of pre- senting a bouquet of flowers to the Princess Victoria. A guard of honour of the 4th Battalion South Lancashire Regiment will be drawn up at the place of " presentation. Leave Warrington (Sankey Bridges) 6.20 passing slowly through Rainhill 6.30. - Arrive Preseot (Railway Bridges 6.45.) Pass slowly through Prescot, stopping to allow Chairman of the Prescot Urban District Council to be presented. Arrive Knowsley 7 p.m The procession will eater the Town Hall grounds by the East Gate and after the presentations will leave by the West Gate. The whole route will be closed to vehicular traffic at 4.30 p.m. and vehicles will not be allowed to cross the route after 4 p.m. Buttermarket-street will also be closed to pedestrians at 4.45 p.m. Householders and persons holding tickets to pass along this street will not be allowed to stand on the footpaths. The following streets and passages open- ing on to Buttermarket-street will be closed and barricaded :

Percival's-fold, Hill.street, Wint-passage, passage leading from "Crown and Sceptre" yard, Upper Bank-street, Cloth Hall-yard and Cropper's-brow. Persons holding tickets for any of the stands must take their seats not later than 5 p.m. No person will be admitted to the Town Hall grounds unless provided with a ticket of admission.

No camera will be allowed in any street except on the footpaths. No camera will be permitted on the stands at the Town Hall.

Photographers are requested not to obtrude on their Majesties. The procession will be headed by Mounted Police. The line of route will be preserved by the 4th Battalion South Lancashire Regiment and a detach- ment of the Bth and 40th Regimental District. The Warrington Ambulance *Corps will be on duty along the line of route. Order will be preserved by the full strength of the Warrington Police Force, assisted by detachments of police from the Cheshire Constabulary, Chester, St. Helens and Wigan.

THE COST.

The Finance and Executive Committee of the Reception Committee met on Thursday and resolved to recommend the Town Council to grant £1,200 in the form of salary to the Mayor to be devoted to the expenditure on the forthcoming Royal visit. At a special meeting on Monday evening next the Town Council will be asked to vote this sum.

===== 0002601_19090703_art0125.txt | included=true | included =====
THE DECORATIONS.

Warrington will indeed be a "Beautiful War- rington" on Tuesday, for the decorations will be the most lavish and artistic ever seen in the town. As already announced, the route has been divided into three sections for decorative purposes, each being allotted to a separate contractor. Section No. 1 (from Bruche Bridge to Mersey-street) is in the hands of Messrs. C. T. Brock and Co. of Crystal Palace fame, through their local agent, M;. T. Prince, Horsemarket-street. When the Royal visitors arrive at the borough boundary the first object to attract their attention will be a handsome arch of lattis work, which will span the roadway at Bruche Bridge. The arch will be painted green and will have eight pilasters, surmounted with natural flowers and connected with floral garlands. The word "Welcome" will be inscribed in gold letteys on each side of the centre arch. Each telegraph post will be ornamented with trophies and banners, with pedestals containing flowers. From Bruche Bridge to the Cemetery there will be a long line of Venetian masts, festooned with garlands, streamers, lines of flags and floral designs, each pole being embellished with shields and trophy of flags. At the Cemetery, gates there will be two triumphal arches, with appropriate mottoes, one spanning Manchester-road and the other Padgate-lane. Each electric standard on this section will be suitably qrnamented and from the arm a basket of flowers will be suspended. About nine feet from the base of the standard will be a trophy of flags. Between the standards elaborate festoons will add a further touch of colour, and in Church-street there will be another avenue of Venetian masts. The general effect promises to be most beautiful. The work is being carried out under the supervision of Mr. J. H. Sharpe.

An attractive feature of the decoratiOn of No. 2 Section (Mersey-street to the Town Hall) will be an. arch in the form of a castle gate in Buttermarket- street. In the narrow portion of Sankey-street mottoes with garlands of artificial flowers will, span the road- way, and in the • wider part of the thoroughfare Venetian masts will be erected on either' side, with garlands of artificial flowers suspended, the whole forming a floral avenue in which the introduction of hanging baskets will have a pleasing effect. At the Market Gate corner of Buttermarket-street a large floral crown will be suspended, with garlands con- necting from the crown to the buildings. The whole of this section is entrusted to Gametts, Warrington. On Section 3 (Crosfield-street to Sankey Bridges) the elecrtic standards will be linktd up with longitudinal swags of streamers. Each post will be decorated with a shield and trophy of five flags and banners, festooned with thick floral garlands and provided with hanging baskets of artificial flowers.

All goods sold by us are manufactured in factories under our own control and supervision, from exclusive designs. We can thus guarantee to our Customers a well made article of useful EIS well as artistic design and substantial manufacture.

R PRICES IT THE POCKET. By controlling our manufactories we place the goods on the Market bearing one profit only—the goods going direct from us, the manufacturers, to our customer, the consumer—bearing only the one profit and not two or three as charged elsewhere.
